Recommended Regulatory Action Approval

Executive Summary
The Applicant (CSL Behring) provided a Complete Response (CR) resubmission to biologics
licensing application (BLA) 761367 for garadacimab, a fully human IgG4 monoclonal
antibody that targets the catalytic subunit of Factor XIIa, for the proposed indication of
prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adult and adolescent
patients 12 years and older. The proposed recommended dosage of garadacimab is a
400mg subcutaneous (SC) injection loading dose followed by 200mg SC injection every
month. HAE is a rare, autosomal dominant genetic condition, caused by reduced or absent
functional C1 esterase inhibitor (C1-INH), which is clinically characterized by episodic and
unpredictable attacks of angioedema involving the skin and/or submucosal surfaces,
including the gastrointestinal tract, genitourinary tract, and/or throat and upper airway, which
can be potentially life-threatening. Garadacimab, an FXIIa inhibitor, represents a novel
therapeutic target for HAE.
After an initial refusal to file due to unavailability of rabbit pyrogen test results, the original
BLA application was resubmitted on October 13, 2023, and filed on December 12, 2023.
The submitted clinical program was adequate to support the efficacy and safety of
garadacimab for the proposed indication. The BLA included the appropriate preapproval
nonclinical and clinical pharmacology studies. The pharmacology/toxicology, clinical
1

Reference ID: 5602056

microbial control and sterility assurance strategy of the DP manufacturing process supported
consistent manufacture of a sterile product. The product quality data supported adequate
control to ensure consistent product quality and process performance. Lastly, the postapproval annual stability protocol and stability commitment were acceptable. CDRH
performed a consult review for the device technical engineering and facilities, and
recommends approval. The BLA is recommended for approval from the product quality,
facility, microbiology, and sterility assurance perspectives. Refer to the OPQ Executive
Summary dated May 20, 2025,4 for additional information.
Pharmacology/Toxicology
No new nonclinical data were submitted or required in this CR resubmission. The updated
proposed PI is acceptable. No pharmacology/toxicology issues preclude approval.
Clinical Pharmacology
No new clinical pharmacology data were provided in this CR resubmission. On
resubmission, the Applicant addressed clinical pharmacology comments in the proposed PI.
These included changes to Sections 7 (Drug Interactions), 12.2 (Pharmacodynamics), and
12.3 (Pharmacokinetics). The updated proposed PI is acceptable from a clinical
pharmacology perspective. As concluded during the initial review cycle, no issues preclude
approval from a
APPEARS THIS
clinical pharmacology perspective.
WAY ON

ORIGINAL
Clinical/Statistics
The initial BLA submission included results from a single, adequate and well-controlled
phase 3 trial (Trial 3001). This trial was a 6-month, randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled trial evaluating 64 adult and adolescent subjects with HAE Type I and II who
received either garadacimab 200mg or placebo monthly.
Trial 3001 met its primary endpoint, demonstrating a statistically significant reduction in the
time-normalized number of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks per month. This endpoint is
an established and clinically meaningful outcome for HAE prophylaxis development
programs. The garadacimab group showed a mean of 0.27 attacks per month (95% CI:
0.05, 0.49) compared to 2.01 attacks per month (95% CI: 1.44, 2.57) in the placebo group.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness of garadacimab in patients with HAE was established
using data from Trial 3001 and confirmatory evidence based on mechanistic data. The
available safety data demonstrated that garadacimab is safe for its intended use.
In the BLA resubmission, no new efficacy data were required or submitted. The Applicant
provided updated exposure and safety datasets through June 15, 2024 (previous data cutoff
was May 22, 2023). The clinical review team analyzed the updated datasets and modified
the drug exposure and frequency of injection site reactions in Section 6 of the PI,
accordingly. Section 14 of the updated proposed PI is acceptable from the statistical and

Andembry / garadacimab

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
The Applicant, CSL Behring, submitted a BLA for garadacimab (proposed tradename:
ANDEMBRY), a fully human IgG4 monoclonal antibody that targets the catalytic subunit of
Factor XIIa, for prophylaxis to prevent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adult and
adolescent patients 12 years and older at a dose of 200 mg subcutaneous (SC) monthly following
a 400 mg SC loading dose. The BLA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. The
clinical, statistical, clinical pharmacology, and pharmacology/toxicology review teams did not
identify any issues that preclude approval. However, the Office of Product Quality and Office of
Pharmaceutical Manufacturing Assessment review teams have determined that the data
submitted in this application are not sufficient to conclude that the manufacture of garadacimab
is well-controlled and will lead to a product that is pure and potent, and therefore, recommend a
complete response.
The substantial evidence of effectiveness of garadacimab in patients with HAE was established
using data from a single adequate and well-controlled trial (CSL312_3001, hereafter referred to
as trial 3001) and confirmatory evidence (CE) based on mechanistic data. Trial 3001
demonstrated a statistically significant reduction in the frequency of acute HAE attacks with
garadacimab treatment compared to placebo, as measured by the primary endpoint of the timenormalized number of HAE attacks/month over a 6-month Treatment Period. This was supported
by the results of secondary endpoints, such as the number of attack-free subjects, moderate-tosevere HAE attacks, and HAE attacks requiring on-demand therapy. The CE providing strong
mechanistic support includes the well-established pathophysiology of the disease and the
mechanism of action of the therapy. In vitro and in vivo pharmacology data show that
garadacimab only antagonizes FXIIa enzymatic activities, prolongs aPTT clotting time in
standard human plasma, and inhibits FXIIa-mediated kallikrein activity in a dose-dependent
fashion. Extrapolation of efficacy from adults with HAE to adolescent patients 12 to 17 years of
age with HAE is supported by the identical disease pathophysiology, mechanism of action,
anticipated treatment response, and clinical manifestations across age groups.
The available safety data demonstrated that garadacimab is safe for its intended use. Common
adverse reactions include nasopharyngitis and abdominal pain. The safety profile for
garadacimab is acceptable for an HAE population and can be adequately managed through
labeling and further evaluated during routine pharmacovigilance.
The BLA includes appropriate preapproval nonclinical studies, and there is no anticipated need
for additional nonclinical studies or clinical pharmacology studies as postmarketing
requirements. The submitted clinical program is adequate to support the efficacy and safety of
garadacimab for the prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE in patients 12 years of age and older.
The clinical review team concludes that the reduction in acute HAE attacks observed with
garadacimab treatment compared to placebo outweighs the risks and that garadacimab will
provide a novel treatment option in the armamentarium for HAE prophylaxis.
The action for this application will be a Complete Response.

Conclusions and Reasons

HAE is a rare, genetic, potentially life-threatening disease
Type I and II hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a rare
characterized by unpredictable, recurrent, self-limited skin and
condition caused by autosomal dominant mutations in the
submucosal swelling attacks. The pathophysiology of the
SERPING1 gene, resulting in deficiency or dysfunction of
disease is well characterized.
C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH) protein.
Approximately 85% of patients have Type I HAE,
characterized by low production of normal C1-INH protein.
The remaining 15% of patients have Type II HAE,
characterized by production of dysfunctional C1-INH.
Types I and II have the same clinical phenotypes and
responses to available treatments.
Insufficient quantities of functional C1-INH lead to
dysregulation of the kallikrein-kinin system, a plasma
protease cascade initiated by Factor XII and culminating in
the generation of bradykinin. Uncontrolled bradykinin
production leads to increased vascular permeability and the
cutaneous/submucosal swelling seen in acute HAE attacks.
Acute HAE attacks are sporadic, recurrent, unpredictable
and typically self-resolve after 48-72 h. Attacks often
involve the GI/GU tract, extremities (hands/feet), and
face/upper airway. Attacks are associated with significant
morbidity due to pain, impact on daily functioning, and
disfigurement and can be life-threatening in cases of
laryngeal edema resulting in airway compromise.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
HAE is a rare, autosomal dominant condition, most often caused by pathogenic variants in the
SERPING-1 gene that encodes C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH), a serine protease-inhibitor
involved in the kallikrein-kinin pathway as well as the classical and lectin complement, contact
system/intrinsic coagulation, and fibrinolytic pathways. Under homeostatic circumstances,
activated Factor XII (FXIIa) triggers the kallikrein-kinin system, resulting in the downstream
production of bradykinin, a potent vasodilator and enhancer of vascular permeability. C1-INH
normally serves as an inhibitory check on the system by inhibiting both kallikrein and FXIIa; in
the absence of sufficient quantities of functional C1-INH, bradykinin production is relatively
unchecked and leads to recurrent swelling (or angioedema) of the skin and submucosa.
Clinically, HAE is characterized by episodic, spontaneous, unpredictable attacks of
nonhistaminergic angioedema involving the skin and submucosal surfaces of the face,
gastrointestinal tract, genitourinary tract, extremities (hands/feet), and/or throat and upper
airway, the last of which can be particularly serious and potentially life-threatening. Several
products are approved for the prophylaxis of HAE attacks as well as for the treatment of acute
HAE attacks; however, FXIIa represents a novel therapeutic target.
To support the BLA for garadacimab, the Applicant submitted the results of a single, adequate
and well-controlled phase 3 trial, 3001. Trial 3001 was a 6-month, randomized, double-blind,
placebo-controlled trial evaluating 64 adult and adolescent subjects with HAE Type I or II who
received garadacimab 200 mg or placebo SC every month. The primary efficacy endpoint was
the time-normalized number of investigator-confirmed HAE attacks per month, an established,
clinically meaningful outcome for HAE prophylaxis development programs. Trial 3001 met its
primary endpoint, demonstrating a statistically significant and clinically meaningful reduction in
the number of HAE attacks per month in the garadacimab group (mean=0.27; 95% CI=0.05,
0.49) compared to the placebo group (mean=2.01; 95% CI=1.44, 2.57). Secondary endpoints—
such as the proportion of subjects with a ≥50% reduction in attacks and the numbers of attackfree subjects, moderate-to-severe HAE attacks, and HAE attacks requiring acute on-demand
treatment—demonstrated results that were supportive of the primary endpoint, all favoring
garadacimab treatment.
Given the rarity of HAE, a single adequate and well-controlled trial with support from CE is
considered sufficient for demonstration of substantial evidence of effectiveness for the proposed
indication. The CE consists of strong mechanistic data from the well-understood
pathophysiology of this genetic disease and the mechanism of action of the drug. As described
above, the kallikrein-kinin pathway and its components, and the mechanism by which pathologic
variants in the SERPING-1 gene lead to bradykinin overproduction and the clinical
manifestations of HAE have been well-characterized. Garadacimab inhibits FXIIa, a key
protease in the activation of the contact/kallikrein-kinin system pathway, and thereby reduces the
downstream production of bradykinin. As bradykinin is the main mediator of angioedema in
HAE, and there are no known alternative pathways for bradykinin production in C1-INH–
deficient patients (i.e., with Types I and II HAE), inhibition of FXIIa and its activation of the
kallikrein-kinin pathway is expected to result in fewer angioedema episodes.
The safety assessment of garadacimab revealed no concerning safety signals that would preclude
approval. No deaths occurred in the clinical development program. Serious adverse events,
6
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
severe adverse events, and adverse events leading to treatment discontinuation were rare with no
major imbalances between treatment groups. Overall, treatment-emergent adverse events were
predominantly mild or moderate in severity, with the most common adverse reactions being
nasopharyngitis and abdominal pain. As with other HAE products that target the contact
system/kallikrein-kinin pathway, prolongation of the coagulation parameters activated partial
thromboplastin time and prothrombin time were asymptomatic and not associated with an
increased risk of bleeding or thromboembolism. Potential risks can be addressed through
labeling and routine pharmacovigilance.
Although trial 3001 enrolled six adolescent subjects (four garadacimab, two placebo), the
efficacy and safety of garadacimab in adolescent patients 12 to <18 years of age is primarily
based on extrapolation from adults and matching pharmacokinetic exposure. Extrapolation is
supported by the same underlying pathophysiology and clinical manifestations of disease,
mechanism of action of garadacimab, and similar expected response to treatment across age
groups.
Overall, the review team concludes that the benefit-risk assessment is favorable and supports the
approval of garadacimab 200 mg SC monthly for the proposed indication of prophylaxis to
prevent HAE attacks in adults and adolescents 12 years of age and above.

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
This review serves as the interdisciplinary assessment for BLA 761367. Garadacimab is a new
molecular entity, first-in-class, fully human IgG4 monoclonal antibody targeting the catalytic
subunit of activated Factor XII (FXIIa). The proposed indication is for prophylaxis to prevent
attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and
older. The proposed dosage is an initial loading dose of 400 mg administered subcutaneously
(SC) on the first day of treatment followed by a maintenance dose of 200 mg SC every month.
HAE is a rare genetic condition with an estimated prevalence of 1 in 10,000 to 50,000 people
(~6000 to 30,000 in the United States) (USHAA 2024). The overwhelming majority of patients
with HAE have pathogenic variants in the SERPING-1 gene that encodes C1-esterase inhibitor
(C1-INH), resulting in either low levels of C1-INH (Type I) or normal levels of dysfunctional
C1-INH (Type II) (Busse et al. 2021). A very small fraction of patients has Type III HAE caused
by other genetic mutations (e.g., Factor XII (FXII), angiopoietin-1, plasminogen (PLG)) and
exhibit normal levels and function of C1-INH. The inheritance of HAE is autosomal dominant;
however, approximately 25% of patients have de novo mutations. Patients with HAE experience
recurrent episodes of swelling in the hands/feet, genitals, gastrointestinal tract, face and/or throat
that typically lasts for 2 to 3 days, but occasionally up to 5 days. Symptom onset is typically in
childhood, with 50% of patients experiencing angioedema episodes by age 10 years, and
symptoms usually increase after puberty. There is a high degree of inter- and intrapatient
variability in both the frequency and severity of attacks. Characteristically, HAE symptoms do
not respond to mast-cell targeted treatments such as antihistamines, corticosteroids, or
epinephrine. Pharmacologic treatment of HAE consists of access to on-demand therapies to treat
acute episodes of angioedema and consideration for long-term prophylaxis on an individual
basis. Approved therapies for prophylaxis and on-demand treatment of acute attacks generally
consist of products that target the kallikrein-kinin pathway (i.e., C1-INH replacements, plasma
kallikrein inhibitors, bradykinin B2-receptor antagonists). Danazol, an attenuated androgen, is
also approved for prophylaxis, although its mechanism of action in HAE is unclear.
Garadacimab represents a novel therapeutic for HAE.
The Applicant submitted an IND application (IND 139936) in 2018 to investigate garadacimab
for the prophylaxis of HAE attacks. Garadacimab received Fast Track Designation on December
14, 2021, and orphan drug designation on April 30, 2020. The Agency refused to file the initial
BLA submission on August 28, 2023, due to a lack of the required Rabbit Pyrogen Test (21 CFR
610.13). The Applicant resubmitted the BLA on October 13, 2023, after addressing the
deficiencies outlined in the Refuse-to-File letter. The application was subsequently filed and is
the subject of this review. Refer to Section 13 for the complete regulatory history.
During the course of the application review, the review team identified two key efficacy review
issues and three key safety review issues, as listed below. In-depth analyses and assessments of
the efficacy and safety review issues can be found in Sections 6.3 and 7.7, respectively.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Garadacimab was shown to only antagonize FXIIa enzymatic activities, displaying 100%
inhibition of FXIIa catalytic activity for both the α and β fragments, meaning that zymogen FXII
functions are negligibly impacted. In an in vitro chromogenic assay, garadacimab inhibited the
enzymatic activity of human plasma-derived FXIIa and βFXIIa at equivalent levels (half
maximal inhibitory concentration~15nM). Garadacimab prolonged the activated partial
thromboplastin time (aPTT) clotting time in standard human plasma.

11
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
Garadacimab did not significantly inhibit other serum proteases tested when incubated at an
enzyme: antibody ratio of up to 1:500 and exhibited half maximal inhibitory concentration
values >1μM for all enzymes tested.
In an in vivo angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor (captopril)-induced edema murine model,
garadacimab significantly inhibited the angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor-induced
increase of vascular permeability. Complete ablation of vascular leakage was observed at doses
of ≥0.5 mg/kg.
The established pharmacologic class for garadacimab has been designated as “FXIIa inhibitor”
(monoclonal antibody) based on the in vitro and in vivo pharmacology data.

Mechanism of action
Garadacimab is a fully human IgG4/lambda recombinant monoclonal antibody
(molecular weight 148 kDa) that binds to the catalytic domain of activated
Factor XII (FXIIa) and inhibits its catalytic activity. Inhibition of FXIIa prevents
the activation of pre-kallikrein to kallikrein and the generation of bradykinin,
which is associated with inflammation and swelling in HAE attacks, thus
blocking the cascade of events leading to an HAE attack.

Clinical and nonclinical ECG findings are consistent with the fact that

are not expected to cause concentration-dependent prolongation of the QTc
interval. However, ECG data collected by the Applicant cannot serve as a
substitute for a thorough QT study. As garadacimab is a monoclonal antibody,
a thorough QT/QTc study is not warranted. For additional details, refer to the
QT-IRT review in DARRTS dated April 25, 2024.

Exposure-response analyses for efficacy based on the relative risk of an HAE
attack indicated that dosages ≥200 mg SC once monthly are predicted to yield
steady-state plasma trough concentrations associated with a 90% reduction in
HAE attack rate compared to placebo. Increasing the dose beyond 200 mg is
not predicted to result in significant further reductions in HAE attack rate.
Trial 2001 evaluated SC dosing regimens up to 600 mg Q4W in patients with
HAE. In healthy subjects, single IV doses up to 10 mg/kg (median dose of
879 mg) were studied.
Garadacimab exposure increased in a dose-proportional manner between IV
doses of 0.1 and 10 mg/kg and SC doses of 1 and 10 mg/kg.
No additional accumulation was observed at steady state following the
proposed 400 mg loading dose with 200 mg monthly maintenance dosing
regimen.
Steady state is achieved by the third dose (i.e., after SC administration of the
initial loading dose of 400 mg and the first 200 mg dose).
In early clinical trials (i.e., 1001, 1003, and 2001), a 100 mg/mL formulation in a
vialed presentation was used. The remaining studies used the to-be-marketed
170 mg/mL formulation in a PFS presentation. A comparison of steady state
trough concentrations across trials 2001 (100 mg/mL) and 3001 and
study 3002 (170 mg/mL) indicated that formulation has no impact on
garadacimab PK.

The final, to-be-marketed formulation in the PFS-NSD presentation was used
in the pivotal clinical trial (3001). The proposed AI presentation was not used in
trial 3001. The PFS-NSD and AI presentations were bridged in relative
bioavailability study 1004 and bioequivalence was demonstrated.

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight is a significant covariate affecting the PK of garadacimab such
that garadacimab exposure is increased in patients with lower body weight.
Body weight-based differences in exposures are not clinically relevant and no
dose adjustments based on body weight are recommended.
Age (12 to 73 years) did not meaningfully influence the PK of garadacimab
No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal
impairment on the PK of garadacimab. Per population PK analysis, renal
impairment (eGFR ≥90 mL/min/1.73 m2 [normal, N=145]; 60 to
<90 mL/min/1.73 m2 [mild, N=26]; and 30 to <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 [moderate,
N=2]) did not impact the PK of garadacimab.
No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of hepatic
impairment on the PK of garadacimab. Per population PK analysis, bilirubin (4
to 13µmol/L – 10th and 90th percentiles, respectively, in the observed dataset)
did not significantly impact garadacimab clearance.

Not applicable
Not applicable
Immunogenicity
The immunogenicity assay was a bridging assay that used an acid dissociation
treatment step. Acid-treated samples were incubated in the presence of
biotinylated and sulfo-tagged garadacimab, which form bridging complexes
with ADAs. A tiered approach was used in which samples were screened for
ADAs, confirmed positive, and then evaluated for titers.
In pivotal trial 3001, the ADA incidence was 2.6% (1/39) in patients who
received garadacimab during the 6-month Treatment Period. Among all
patients with HAE who received the proposed garadacimab dosing regimen in
trial 3001 and study 3002, the incidence of treatment-emergent ADAs was
3.9% (5/129).
There was no apparent impact of immunogenicity on the PK, efficacy, or safety
of garadacimab. Overall, few patients with HAE tested positive for ADAs,
precluding a definitive assessment of the impact of ADAs.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
The proposed dose of garadacimab is 200 mg SC every month, with an initial 400 mg loading
dose. The dose and dosing regimen were investigated in a phase 2 dose-ranging trial (2001) and
a phase 3 pivotal trial (3001), and by pharmacokinetic (PK) modeling.
Trial 2001
Trial 2001, described in detail in Section 16.2, evaluated four dosing regimens: 75 mg SC,
200 mg SC, and 600 mg SC every 4 weeks (Q4W) with different intravenous (IV) loading doses
(40, 100, and 300 mg, respectively), and 400 mg SC every 2 weeks (open label). The primary
endpoint of time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was compared to placebo for the
200 mg and 600 mg treatment arms (see Table 117). The primary endpoint was also compared
exploratorily between the 200 mg and 600 mg arms. Both the 200 mg and 600 mg arms met the
primary objective and showed significant reductions in the time-normalized number of HAE
attacks, but injection site reactions (ISRs) were more common in the 600 mg arm compared to
the 200 mg arm. In March 2020, a protocol amendment was submitted by the Applicant, and all
subjects with C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE were switched to the 200 mg arm.
Clinical and PK data from trial 2001 were used to support dose selection for trial 3001. The
garadacimab concentration-effect relationships were explored based on the relative risk of an
HAE attack using maximal-efficacy model. The model-predicted relative risk of an HAE attack
as a function of steady-state garadacimab plasma trough concentration is shown in Figure 1.
Model predictions evaluated dosing regimens in which garadacimab was administered SC every
month and included administration of an initial loading dose of twofold the monthly dose.
Results indicated that dosages ≥200 mg administered SC once monthly yielded a steady-state
median plasma trough concentration associated with a ~90% reduction, on average, in HAE
attack rate compared to placebo. Model predictions also indicated that a plateau phase for
reduction of HAE attack rate was reached following the 200-mg dose.

15
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
After obtaining informed consent (or assent for minors), subjects entered the Screening Period to
determine eligibility for study enrollment. Individuals with FXII/PLG (Type III) HAE were not
enrolled. Screened subjects who met all the inclusion criteria, and none of the exclusion criteria,
entered the Run-in Period (see Section 6.2.2 for the key inclusion and exclusion criteria). If a
subject did not meet all the criteria for entering the Run-in Period within 30 days, the subject
could be rescreened once.
Following screening, subjects entered the Run-in Period, which lasted at least 1 month and up to
2 months, to confirm their underlying disease status and assess their eligibility for participation
in the Treatment Period. Subjects had to experience ≥2 HAE attacks during the Run-in Period to
enter the Treatment Period. Subjects who experienced ≥2 HAE attacks during the required first
month could exit the Run-in Period and enter the Treatment Period. Subjects were not allowed to
take prophylaxis during the Run-in Period. Adults on HAE prophylaxis had a minimum 2-week
(for C1-INH products, androgens, and antifibrinolytics) or 3-month (for lanadelumab) Washout
Period before entering the Run-in Period (subjects not on prophylaxis did not require a Washout
Period). Adolescents on routine (long-term) HAE prophylaxis were excluded from the trial.
In the Treatment Period, 64 eligible participants were randomized 3:2 to receive 200 mg
garadacimab or matching placebo once monthly for 6 months. Randomization accounted for age
(≤17 years; >17 years) and, for adults, baseline attack rate, defined as the time-normalized
number of HAE attacks per month during the Run-in Period (1 to <3; ≥3).
For details regarding the collection and reporting of HAE data, and the investigator confirmation
of HAE attacks, see Section 15.1.1.
The overall design of the trial is consistent with previous programs for HAE prophylaxis. The
duration of the Treatment Period (6 months) is acceptable for this program.
Participants who completed the Treatment Period were eligible to enroll in a phase 3b, OLE
study (3002; see Sections 7.6.2 and 16.2).
Primary Objective and Endpoint
The primary objective of this trial was to evaluate the efficacy of garadacimab as prophylaxis to
prevent HAE attacks in subjects with C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE. The primary efficacy
endpoint was the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the Treatment
Period compared to matched placebo (only investigator-confirmed HAE attacks, as defined in
Section 15.1.1, were included). The primary endpoint was calculated per subject as:
[number of HAE attacks ÷ length of subject treatment in days] × 30.4375
Length of subject treatment (in days) was calculated as:
[date of Study Visit Day 182 or the End-of-Study date (whichever occurred first)
− date of Study Visit Day 1 of Treatment Period + 1]
Secondary and Exploratory Objectives and Endpoints
The secondary trial objectives included characterizing the clinical efficacy of garadacimab in the
prophylactic treatment of HAE. Only investigator-confirmed HAE attacks, as defined in

19
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
Section 15.1.1, were included in the efficacy evaluation. The key secondary efficacy endpoints
were:


Percentage reduction in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during
the Treatment Period.



Number of subjects who did not experience an HAE attack (i.e., were attack-free) in the first
3 months of the Treatment Period.



Percentage of subjects with good or excellent responses to the Subject’s Global Assessment
of Response to Therapy (SGART) at the end of the Treatment Period.

The first key secondary endpoint above was calculated as:
100 × [(mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for garadacimab − mean
time-normalized number of HAE attacks for placebo) ÷ mean time-normalized
number of HAE attacks for placebo].
The other secondary efficacy endpoints were:


Percentage reduction (≥50%, ≥70%, ≥90%, and 100% [attack-free]) in the time-normalized
number of HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period compared to the
Run-in Period.



Time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy during the
Treatment Period.



Time-normalized number of moderate or severe HAE attacks per month during the
Treatment Period.

HAE attack severity was assessed by the investigator based on the subject’s description of the
attack and reflected the maximum intensity of the attack. The Applicant specified the following
qualifiers for investigator determination of HAE attack severity in the clinical study protocol
(dated August 4, 2020), though they could have varied between subjects and attacks:
Mild:


Little to no effect on daily activities.



Use of HAE on-demand therapy to treat the attack may not be necessary.



Other concomitant medication (e.g., analgesics) may be used to treat attack symptoms.

Moderate:


Causes daily activities to be difficult.



Some assistance may be needed to complete daily activities.



Use of HAE on-demand therapy to treat the attack is probable.

Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
Severe:


Causes marked limitation of daily activities.



Medical assistance and intervention may be required, including at clinic emergency room
visit or hospitalization.



HAE on-demand therapy is used to treat the attack.

An exploratory objective of this trial was to further evaluate the efficacy and quality of life
associated with the use of garadacimab in subjects with C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE. This
included analyzing an exploratory endpoint of change in subject total and domain scores from
the angioedema quality of life (AE-QoL) questionnaire from baseline to Day 182 by treatment
arm.

6.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial 3001
Key eligibility criteria are summarized below. The trial enrolled subjects ≥12 years of age with
clinically confirmed HAE and an attack frequency that would likely warrant prophylactic HAE
therapy. The inclusion and exclusion criteria are acceptable and consistent with those used in
previous studies of approved products for HAE prophylaxis.
Key Inclusion Criteria


Male or female ≥12 years of age at the time of informed consent.



Diagnosed with clinically confirmed C1-INH (Type I or Type II) HAE:
– Documented clinical history consistent with HAE (SC or mucosal, nonpruritic swelling
episodes without accompanying urticaria), and
– C1-INH antigen and/or functional activity ≤50% of normal, and
– C4 antigen concentration below the lower limit of the reference range.



Experienced ≥3 HAE attacks during the 3 months before Screening (subjects taking any
prophylactic HAE therapy prior to Screening could present documentation of ≥3 HAE
attacks during the three consecutive months before commencing the prophylactic therapy).

Key Exclusion Criteria


Concomitant diagnosis of another form of angioedema, such as idiopathic or acquired
angioedema, recurrent angioedema associated with urticaria, or FXII/PLG (Type III) HAE.



For adult subjects: use of C1-INH products, androgens, antifibrinolytics, or other small
molecule medications for routine prophylaxis against HAE attacks within 2 weeks prior to
the Run-in Period.



For adolescent subjects (12 to 17 years of age): use of long-term prophylactic therapy for
HAE before Screening.



Use of a monoclonal antibody, such as lanadelumab, within 3 months prior to the
Run-in Period.

6.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial 3001
The final statistical analysis plan (statistical analysis plan (SAP); version 1.0) is dated April 21,
2021. For details about the sample size calculation for this trial, see Section 15.1.3. The
following analysis sets were defined by the Applicant:
Screened Analysis Set: All subjects who provided written informed consent (or assent for
minors) and who underwent study screening procedures.
Intention-to-Treat Analysis Set: All subjects in the Screened Analysis Set who were randomized.
The intent-to-treat (ITT) Analysis Set was analyzed using the treatment to which the subject was
randomized, regardless of the treatment actually received. The ITT Analysis Set was the main set
used for efficacy evaluation.
Safety Analysis Set: All subjects in the ITT Analysis Set who received at least one dose of
investigational product. The Safety Analysis Set was analyzed using the actual treatment
received and was the main set used for safety evaluation.
Per Protocol Analysis Set: All subjects in the ITT Analysis Set who received at least one dose of
investigational product and complied with the protocol. Decisions regarding exclusion from the
Per Protocol Analysis Set were made and documented prior to unblinding.
Use of the phrase “complied with the protocol” in the definition for the Per Protocol Analysis Set
is vague, and the Applicant did not provide any further details. However, there was only one
major protocol violation in the trial (one subject missed a visit and then withdrew consent).
6.2.3.1. Testing Hierarchy and Multiplicity Control, Trial 3001
Four hypothesis tests (one for the primary endpoint [H01] and three for the key secondary
endpoints [H02, H03, and H04]) were performed hierarchically at a two-sided alpha of 5%.
Below is a description of the four null hypotheses associated with these four tests, per the
Applicant’s final SAP:
1. H01: The time-normalized numbers of HAE attacks in the first 6-month time period of the
Active Arm and in the Placebo Arm period are equal.
2. H02: The percentage reductions in the mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for the
6 months of the Active Arm compared to the 6-month Placebo Arm period are equal to zero.
3. H03: The number of subjects who do not experience a HAE attack in the first 3 months of the
Active Arm and the first 3-month Placebo Period are equal.
4. H04: The percentage of subjects with good or excellent responses to the SGART at the end of
the Treatment Period (Day 182) are equal for subjects treated with garadacimab and placebo.
From the final SAP, the general flow of the hypothesis testing was as follows:
The null hypothesis associated with the primary endpoint (H01) was tested first at a two-sided
alpha of 5%. If and only if the resulting two-sided p-value was <0.05, testing of H02 would be
performed. If and only if H02 was rejected (with a two-sided p-value <0.05), H03 would be
tested. If and only if H03 was rejected (with a two-sided p-value <0.05), H04 would be tested at
a two-sided alpha of 5%.

22
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
The definition for H02 was unclear in the trial protocol (dated August 4, 2020). Prior to database
lock, the Applicant modified H02 in the final SAP, but it included two different specifications
for H02. In the Applicant’s response (dated June 27, 2024) to an FDA Information Request (IR),
it was clarified that H02 was defined as written above. In a separate IR response (dated July 23,
2024), the Applicant also clarified that H02 was evaluating the percentage reduction in the
means relative to placebo during the Treatment Period, not the percentage reduction in the means
for each treatment arm relative to the Run-in Period (this was an additional secondary endpoint
outside of the testing hierarchy). H01 and H02 were essentially evaluating different summary
measures of the same endpoint; no rationale was provided for including H02 in the testing
hierarchy. The Applicant may have wanted to evaluate the percentage reduction for increased
clinical interpretability (since the results of a Wilcoxon rank sum test are not as straightforward)
and labeling purposes.
6.2.3.2. Analysis of the Primary Endpoint, Trial 3001
The Applicant’s primary objective was to assess the treatment effect of garadacimab during the
6-month Treatment Period (from Day 1 [first study drug administration] through Day 182) while
subjects were allowed to treat HAE attacks with on-demand therapy.
Primary Estimand
The Applicant defined the following attributes for the primary estimand:


Treatment Condition: Monthly treatment of garadacimab or placebo.



Population: The target subject population defined by the study eligibility criteria and who
were randomized (the ITT Analysis Set).



Variable: Time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during treatment from Day 1
through Day 182.



Intercurrent Events: All observed values were used regardless of the occurrence of the
following intercurrent events (treatment policy strategy):
– Administration of on-demand therapy in addition to prophylactic treatment with
garadacimab.
– Prohibited concomitant medications due or not due to coronavirus disease 2019
(COVID-19) or COVID-19 vaccination.
– Noncompliance to treatment due or not due to COVID-19 or COVID-19 vaccination.
– Early study discontinuation due or not due to COVID-19.
The intercurrent event of early study discontinuation due or not due to COVID-19 was
removed prior to database lock per FDA feedback, as the Agency did not consider this to
be an intercurrent event. In the final SAP, the Applicant prespecified that subjects who
discontinue treatment or the study but are on study treatment for at least 30 days would
be included in the primary analysis. Additionally, no subjects discontinued treatment or
the study as a result of COVID-19.



Population-Level Summary: Median time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month
during treatment from Day 1 through Day 182.
23

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
Primary Analysis
The analysis method for evaluating the primary endpoint in the ITT Analysis Set was a twosided Wilcoxon rank sum test at alpha=5%. The population-level summary specified in the
Applicant’s primary estimand was the median, but the Wilcoxon rank sum test is not a median
test.
If a subject discontinued the study early after being in the Treatment Period for the first 30 days
continuously, observation was terminated by the Early Termination Visit. Based on the data from
the phase 2 trial (2001), it was assumed that the attack rate for subjects who discontinued the
study after Study Day 30 and before the end of Month 6 would be comparable to the attack rate
among subjects who completed the entire 6-month Treatment Period. Therefore, the primary
endpoint was considered measurable and not missing for these subjects (the time-normalized
number of HAE attacks was calculated based on the HAE attacks reported until the subject’s
Early Termination Visit).
If a subject discontinued treatment due or not due to COVID-19 but continued participation in
the study for more than 30 days beyond the last study drug administration, the HAE attacks until
the last study drug administration plus 30 days were included in the calculation of the primary
endpoint (the study drug was administered monthly). For these subjects, length of treatment (in
days) was calculated as:
[date of last study drug administration − date of Study Visit Day 1 of the Treatment Period + 31].
Missing Data
The final SAP prespecified that the primary endpoint (the time-normalized number of HAE
attacks per month) would only be considered missing for subjects who discontinued study
treatment within 30 days after the first study drug administration.
Sensitivity Analyses
The sensitivity analysis below was specified in the Applicant’s final SAP.
To evaluate the robustness of the primary efficacy results, the time-normalized number of HAE
attacks per month was compared between the garadacimab and placebo arms using a generalized
linear model for count data assuming a Poisson distribution with the log link function and
Pearson chi-squared scaling of standard errors to account for potential overdispersion. The model
included treatment arm and baseline attack rate (the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per
month during the Run-in Period) as covariates. To account for differences in treatment duration
among subjects, the logarithm of subject time on treatment was included as an offset term in the
model.
From this model, the least squares (LS) mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per
month and associated standard errors were estimated for the garadacimab and placebo arms. To
report the mean HAE attack rate per month, the received model estimates were transformed by
the exponential function and scaled by time. The corresponding attack ratio for garadacimab
relative to placebo and its 95% CI were also reported. The estimated attack ratio was used to
calculate the percentage difference in the means as follows: 100% × (attack ratio − 1). The 95%
CI for the percentage difference in means was calculated using the upper and lower confidence
limits obtained for the attack ratio, transformed by subtracting 1 and then multiplying by 100%.
24
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
This sensitivity analysis was applied to the ITT Analysis Set.
Subgroup Analyses
In the final SAP, the Applicant prespecified that if five or more Japanese subjects were enrolled
in the trial, then a descriptive analysis of the primary endpoint (the time-normalized number of
HAE attacks per month) would be conducted for the subset of Japanese subjects (the Applicant
did not provide a rationale for this analysis). Since a total of six Japanese subjects were enrolled
in the trial, the Applicant completed this subgroup analysis and submitted the results for review.
Analyses of the primary endpoint by other clinically relevant subgroups were not prespecified or
submitted by the Applicant. Additional analyses of the primary endpoint by other clinically
relevant subgroups (by age, sex, geographic location, HAE subtype, HAE attack location and
severity, and other races/ethnicities) were completed by the Agency to inform regulatory
decision-making. See Section 6.2.4.6 for the results of subgroup analyses for the primary
endpoint.
6.2.3.3. Analysis of the Key Secondary Endpoints, Trial 3001
The Applicant prespecified the following statistical tests for the three key secondary endpoints:


Percentage reduction in the means of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month
was assessed using a two-sided Wilcoxon rank sum test.



Number of subjects who did not experience an HAE attack (i.e., were attack-free) in the first
3 months of the 6-month Treatment Period was evaluated using a two-sided Fisher exact test.



Percentage of subjects with good or excellent responses to the SGART at the end of the
Treatment Period was assessed using a two-sided chi-squared test.

The first key secondary endpoint above was defined as:
100 × [(mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks for garadacimab − mean
time-normalized number of HAE attacks for placebo) ÷ mean time-normalized
number of HAE attacks for placebo].
Its corresponding hypothesis (H02) was “the percentage reduction in means of the timenormalized number of HAE attacks for the 6 months of the Active Arm compared to the 6-month
Placebo Arm period are equal to zero” (specified in the protocol, and confirmed by the
Applicant’s IR responses dated June 27, 2024, and July 23, 2024). The Wilcoxon rank sum test is
a nonparametric approach to test location shift between two distributions. However, H02 was to
test whether a summary statistic equaled zero, for which the Wilcoxon rank-sum test would not
be applicable. In Section 6.2.4.4, only descriptive statistics have been presented for this endpoint.
Key secondary estimands were not defined by the Applicant.
Missing Data
Secondary endpoints based on HAE attack rate (e.g., the first key secondary endpoint) used the
same missing data assumptions as the primary endpoint (see Missing Data in Section 6.2.3.2).

6.2.3.4. Other Secondary/Exploratory Endpoint Analyses,
Trial 3001
Additional secondary and exploratory endpoints in this trial included:





Percentage reduction (≥50%, ≥70%, ≥90% and 100% [attack-free]) in the time-normalized
number of HAE attacks per month during the Treatment Period compared to the
Run-in Period.
Time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy during the
Treatment Period.
Time-normalized number of moderate or severe HAE attacks per month during the
Treatment Period.
Change from baseline to Day 182 in total and domain scores from the AE-QoL
questionnaire.

Percentage reductions were assessed descriptively by treatment arm, and an exploratory, twosided Fisher exact test was prespecified and conducted for subjects with a 100% reduction only.
The time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month requiring on-demand therapy and the
time-normalized number of moderate or severe HAE attacks per month were evaluated by
treatment arm using exploratory, two-sided Wilcoxon rank-sum tests. The Agency was also
interested in assessing these two endpoints using exploratory Poisson models structured similarly
to the Applicant’s sensitivity analysis for the primary endpoint described in Section 6.2.3.2.
AE-QoL data were collected only from subjects 18 years of age or older and were summarized
descriptively by treatment arm. AE-QoL total and domain scores were calculated as:
100 × [sum of all completed items ÷ maximum possible sum of all completed items].
Estimands were not defined for any of the endpoints above.
Missing Data
Additional secondary and exploratory endpoints based on HAE attack rate used the same missing
data assumptions as the primary endpoint (see Missing Data in Section 6.2.3.2).
For the AE-QoL data, domain scores were not calculated if more than one item was missing for a
domain, and total scores were not calculated if more than four items were missing in total.
Subjects without an AE-QoL domain and/or total score were excluded from the descriptive
summary accordingly.

Section 6.2.1. The results of additional analyses of interest to the Agency are also reported.

6.2.4.2. Baseline Demographics and Clinical Characteristics,
Trial 3001
The demographics and clinical characteristics of the ITT Set at baseline are summarized in

study enrolled more females (59%) than males (41%), which is generally consistent with the

outside the United States (67% of the ITT Set). Additionally, 33% of the ITT Set reported use of

6.2.4.3. Primary Efficacy Results, Trial 3001
The primary efficacy endpoint was the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month
during the 6-month Treatment Period. This endpoint is clinically meaningful and consistent with
previous programs for HAE prophylaxis. Details regarding the reporting, collection, and
investigator confirmation of HAE attacks are provided in Section 15.1.1.
The primary efficacy results are summarized in Table 10; the total number of HAE attacks in the
garadacimab arm was over fourfold lower than in the placebo arm (63 versus 264). The
estimated mean time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month was considerably lower in
the garadacimab arm (mean=0.27; 95% CI=0.05, 0.49) compared to placebo (mean=2.01; 95%
CI=1.44, 2.57), signifying an improvement in the garadacimab arm over placebo. Furthermore,
there was a statistically significant difference (Wilcoxon p-value <0.001) in the rank sums of the
time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month in the garadacimab and placebo arms (i.e.,
the rank sum in the garadacimab arm was significantly lower than the placebo arm, assuming
that a higher rank indicates a worse outcome).

6.2.4.4. Key Secondary Efficacy Results, Trial 3001
The first key secondary endpoint in the testing hierarchy (see Section 6.2.3.1) was the percentage
reduction in the means of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month during the
Treatment Period. H02 was specified to test “the percentage reduction in means of the timenormalized number of HAE attacks for the 6-months of the Active Arm compared to the 6month Placebo Arm period are equal to zero.” The mean time-normalized number of HAE
attacks per month during the 6-month Treatment Period was 0.27 for the garadacimab arm and
2.01 for the placebo arm (Table 10). From the Clinical Study Report (dated October 6, 2022), the
percentage reduction in the mean relative to placebo was 86.5% (95% CI=57.8%, 95.7%).
As mentioned in Section 6.2.3.3, the Wilcoxon rank sum test is a nonparametric approach to test
location shift between two distributions and is not applicable to test H02 for “the percentage
reduction in means of the time-normalized number of HAE attacks for the 6-months of the
Active Arm compared to the 6-month Placebo Arm period are equal to zero.” Furthermore, from
the Applicant-submitted software program for analysis of this endpoint, the p-value from the
Wilcoxon rank sum test for the primary endpoint was reported as the p-value for this endpoint,
which is not appropriate. Therefore, in this review, no p-value is reported for this endpoint.
The first key secondary endpoint and the primary endpoint use different descriptive summary
measures (the median for the primary endpoint and the percentage reduction in the means for the
first key secondary endpoint) to evaluate the same endpoint (the time-normalized number of
HAE attacks per month for the 6-month Treatment Period). The first key secondary endpoint
does not provide any new information for evidence of effectiveness of the study drug.

Andembry / garadacimab
listing provided in the clinical study report (CSR). In some instances, the CSR did not reflect all
locations involved in an HAE attack that were recorded in the CRF. For example, the CRF might
have documented the attack location as “eyelids bilaterally, buttock (right), left arm, right arm,
abdomen,” but the CSR line listing only contained the terms “cutaneous, extremities,” omitting
“abdomen.” This discrepancy may affect the accuracy of the subgroup analyses by location but
did not affect the primary efficacy endpoint, as the overall number of HAE attacks reported in
the source documents matched the number reported by the Applicant in the CSR. See Section 23
for additional details.
Overall, the results reported above align with the primary analysis results and support the
effectiveness of garadacimab in reducing HAE attacks, irrespective of attack location and
severity.
Conclusion
While the interpretation of the subgroup analyses above is limited by small sample sizes, the
treatment benefit of garadacimab based on the primary endpoint appears to be similar regardless
of sex, age, race, ethnicity, geographic location, HAE subtype, and HAE attack location and
severity. Furthermore, due to the pathophysiology of HAE being the same across all age groups,
extrapolation of the adult efficacy data to adolescents is clinically reasonable and supported by
PK matching (see Section 15.2.5).

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Patient-Reported Outcome Endpoints
Issue
Determine whether the SGART and the AE-QoL questionnaires are fit-for-purpose for this
(b) (4)
context of use to support regulatory decision-making
.
Background
HAE is characterized by unpredictable, recurrent episodes of angioedema, which greatly impact
quality of life and daily functioning. At the HAE Patient-Focused Drug Development meeting
held at the Agency on September 25, 20171, patients with HAE shared the aspects of the disease
that had the most impact on their lives, including the unpredictability of attack occurrence, the
painful abdominal attacks, the dramatic change swelling had on their physical appearance, and
the emotional toll the disease had on their lives.
The SGART and AE-QoL were used to derive secondary and exploratory endpoints,
(b) (4)
respectively, in trial 3001,
. There are currently no fit-for-purpose patient-reported outcomes (PROs)
for use in HAE prophylaxis clinical trials.

1

https://www.fda.gov/files/about%20fda/published/The-Voice-of-the-Patient---Hereditary-Angioedema.pdf.

41
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
Assessment
The Applicant did not request Agency feedback on the SGART or AE-QoL endpoints during
development, nor did they provide exact copies of the SGART and AE-QoL that they
administered during the study (e.g., electronic screen shots) or any associated training materials
or user manuals for review. The Applicant did not provide a PRO evidence dossier to support the
measurement properties of the SGART and AE-QoL and to establish their fitness-for-purpose for
the context of use. While existing literature demonstrate acceptable psychometric properties of
the AE-QoL (Weller et al. 2012; Weller et al. 2016), this will not replace or rectify problems
with its content validity. In general, the content validity of a clinical outcome assessment needs
to be established prior to establishing its quantitative psychometric properties.
SGART
The SGART is a self-reported instrument completed electronically via a provisioned electronic
device (e.g., a tablet) at the study site to measure a subject’s overall treatment response to the
investigational product based on the question: “Considering all of the ways HAE affects you,
please rate your response to the study medication you were given to prevent HAE attacks during
this Treatment Period.” Specifically, subjects of all ages responded to the single-item question
using a 5-point verbal rating scale on Days 91 and 182, respectively. The five response options
were:


(0) None – worse or no response at all, not acceptable.



(1) Poor – very little response, not acceptable.



(2) Fair – some response, acceptable but could be better.



(3) Good – good response, acceptable.



(4) Excellent – excellent response, as good as can be imagined.

To construct the key secondary endpoint, the SGART asks subjects to respond based on their
experience in the past 6 months, making it susceptible to recall error. A global impression score
may be influenced by factors outside of the disease process, is unable to characterize the drug’s
effect on individual signs/symptoms, and may mask the fact that an overall improvement in score
can be achieved by improvement in some signs/symptoms with concurrent worsening in other
signs/symptoms. In addition, the SGART appears to assess some aspect of treatment satisfaction
based on the phrasing of its item and response options. From a regulatory perspective, it is more
appropriate to assess whether subjects experienced a meaningful improvement in symptoms
rather than satisfaction with treatment. Although the percentage of subjects with good/excellent
SGART responses at the end of the 6-month Treatment Period was a prespecified secondary
endpoint in the testing hierarchy and achieved statistically significant results compared to
(b) (4)
placebo, the SGART does not appear to be content-valid for the context of use
.
AE-QoL
The AE-QoL questionnaire was developed to assess quality of life impairment in recurrent
angioedema patients, including patients with HAE (Weller et al. 2012). It is a 17-item instrument
that uses a 5-point Likert scale with response options of never, rarely, sometimes, often, and very
42
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
often as well as a recall period of the last four weeks. The 17 items within the AE-QoL are
organized into four distinct domains labeled as follows: functioning, fatigue/mood, fears/shame,
and food/nutrition (Table 21).
Table 21. Conceptual Framework of the AE-QoL Questionnaire

Abbreviations, AE-QoL, Angioedema-Quality of Life Questionnaire

Each domain has its own score, and a total score can also be calculated. The items are scored
from 0 to 4, and they are summed to provide raw domain scores, which are then transformed into
linear scores ranging from 0 to 100. The total score is the sum of all 17 items. Higher scores in
the AE-QoL domain and total scores indicate greater levels of impairment of quality of life. In
trial 3001, subjects ≥18 years were asked to complete the AE-QoL via a provisioned electronic
device at the trial site on Days 1, 31, 61, 91, 121, 151, and 182, respectively.
There is inadequate qualitative evidence in Weller et al. (2012) to support the content validity of
the AE-QoL for use in the Applicant’s drug development program. In addition, the domains of
the AE-QoL are not well-defined and reliable to inform regulatory decision-making.
Specifically, for the AE-QoL functioning domain, we note that asking patients about restrictions
43
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
swelling episodes have on their daily lives regardless of whether or not they have actually
experienced swelling episodes during a 4-week period will not provide useful information for
assessing treatment benefit in the context of trial 3001. While the core concepts of diseaserelated symptoms and physical function are reasonable PRO measurement strategies for
demonstrating treatment benefit, emotional impacts (such as anxiety and embarrassment) as
assessed by the AE-QoL fears/shame domain are distal concepts that may be affected by factors
external to HAE disease or effect of the investigational product. In addition, we do not agree that
the 4-week recall period of the AE-QoL is appropriate as patients may not reliably recall the
occurrence of their HAE attacks during this timeframe. As such, we do not believe that the AEQoL is content valid for the context of use.
The Applicant proposed a 6-point change as a minimal clinically important difference in the AEQoL total and domain scores, positioned as exploratory endpoints in trial 3001. In general,
(b) (4)
endpoints positioned as exploratory endpoints and not multiplicity-controlled are
. The Applicant did not conduct anchor-based analyses to interpret
meaningful within-patient score changes in PRO endpoints scores in trial 3001 as no external
anchor scales (e.g., a Patient Global Impression of Severity or Patient Global Impression of
Change) were included in trial 3001. Instead, the Applicant referenced the 6-point minimal
clinically important difference threshold reported in Weller et al. (2016). Given the issues with
the content validity of the AE-QoL, it is premature to evaluate meaningful score changes for the
AE-QoL. While an anchor-based method is the primary method for deriving a range of
thresholds that would constitute a meaningful within-patient change in target PRO scores, it
should be supplemented with empirical cumulative distribution function and probability density
function curves. In addition, estimation of clinically meaningful within-patient change thresholds
should be confirmed in the specific context of the drug development trial. Detailed
recommendations on the conduct of anchor-based analyses, including properties of appropriate
anchor scales, can be found in FDA’s Patient-Focused Drug Development Discussion Document:
Incorporating Clinical Outcome Assessments into Endpoints for Regulatory Decision-Making.2
Conclusion
Based on the evidence available to date and the construction of the instruments, the SGART and
AE-QoL are not well-defined and reliable to inform regulatory decision making and thus, not fit(b) (4) .
for-purpose

6.3.2. Type III HAE
Issue
Determine if there is sufficient evidence to conclude that garadacimab is effective in FXII/PLG
(Type III) HAE patients (or a subset of Type III HAE patients) for the purposes of labeling.
Background
HAE is a rare autosomal disease classified into three subtypes: I, II, and III. HAE is most
commonly caused by variants in the SERPING1 gene, resulting in low or absent levels of C1INH (Type I, most common) or normal to elevated levels of dysfunctional C1-INH (Type II).
2 https://www.fda.gov/media/166830/download

44
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
However, HAE can also present with normal C1-INH (Type III) resulting from variants in other
genes (e.g., F12, PLG, ANGPT1, KNG1, MYOF, HS3ST6); Type III HAE is a very rare,
heterogeneous subtype (Santacroce et al. 2021). Genetic variants in PLG and factor XII (F12) are
the most commonly reported variants in patients with Type III HAE (Bork et al. 2018). In
addition to genetic differences, the F12 and PLG variants also translate to differences in clinical
presentation. For example, estrogen has a markedly aggravating effect on angioedema in F12
HAE, and there is a higher frequency of tongue swelling in PLG HAE3. Erythema marginatum is
occasionally observed in Type I and Type II HAE, but has not been reported for Type III HAE.
Although the three types of HAE share the clinical presentation of episodic angioedema, Type III
HAE exhibits some clinical phenotypic differences, such as predominant face/tongue swelling
and less frequent abdominal attacks, when compared to Type I and Type II HAE.
Garadacimab is a monoclonal antibody targeting the catalytic domain of factor XIIa, blocking its
activation of the kallikrein-kinin system and in turn preventing the proteolytic conversion of
high-molecular-weight kininogen to bradykinin. In Type I and Type II HAE, excess bradykinin
generation has been shown to be the primary mechanism leading to angioedema. In Type III
HAE, the mutations seem to affect the fibrinolytic and kallikrein-kinin system pathways at
different levels which ultimately lead to bradykinin-mediated angioedema; however, the
different gene mutations lead to different biochemical disturbances, resulting in different clinical
features, which has led some authors to suggest that each Type III subtype be considered a
distinct disease entity (Bork et al. 2020).
Currently available HAE products are approved for patients with HAE, but historically clinical
trials have only evaluated subjects with Type I and Type II HAE (due to both the rarity of and
challenges in diagnosing Type III HAE), a fact which is reflected in the descriptions of the study
populations in the product labeling. Although trial 3001 enrolled only subjects with Type I and
Type II HAE, available response data from the subgroup of subjects with Type III HAE enrolled
in the phase 2 trial (2001) are available for evaluation.
Assessment
The Applicant enrolled six subjects with Type III HAE in the phase 2 trial (2001). All six
Type III HAE subjects received open-label garadacimab 600 mg SC Q4W (with a 300 mg IV
loading dose). The Type III HAE subjects did not have a comparator arm for efficacy or safety
assessments in trial 2001. Of the six Type III HAE subjects, three subjects had variants in PLG,
and three had variants in F12. According to an IR response dated April 9, 2024, the three
subjects with PLG variants each had the gain-of-function variant Lys330Glu, and the three
subjects with variants in F12 included Thr328Lys, deletion of 72 base pairs (c.971_1018 +
24del72), or duplication of 18 base pairs (c.892-909dup). Unlike Type I and Type II HAE
subjects enrolled in trial 2001 who received multiple dosing regimens, subjects with Type III
HAE were dosed at the highest garadacimab dose level studied (600 mg Q4W SC) throughout
Treatment Periods 1 and 2 (Figure 65). The three subjects with variants in PLG exhibited less
than a 50% reduction from baseline in HAE attacks; however, One subject experienced a
decrease from three HAE attacks per month during the Run-in Period to 1.67 attacks per month
while on garadacimab treatment. Two of the three subjects with F12 variants responded to
garadacimab (≥50% reduction in HAE attacks) and enrolled in the phase 3b OLE study (3002).
3

https://ojrd.biomedcentral.com/counter/pdf/10.1186/s13023-020-01570-x.pdf.

45
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
One subject with a F12 variant experienced two HAE attacks per month during the
Run-in Period and 2.33 attacks per month while on garadacimab treatment. In comparison, the
Type I and Type II subjects in the 75 mg, 200 mg, and 400 mg garadacimab arms had reductions
of ≥50%. Most Type I and Type II subjects (86%) in the 600 mg garadacimab arm also met this
endpoint. No Type I or Type II subject on placebo achieved a ≥50% reduction. Refer to
Sections 15.3 and 16.2 for more details.
Although the number of subjects with PLG variants studied was small, the lack of response to
garadacimab in some subjects with PLG variants suggests that the development of HAE attacks
in these subjects may occur by a mechanism that bypasses factor XIIa, the target of garadacimab.
This is supported by a report that PLG gain-of-function variants may result in the formation of
bradykinin via downstream mechanisms (Hintze et al. 2022). Additionally, in vivo studies have
shown that the incubation of PLG gain-of-function protein enhanced the breakdown of highmolecular-weight kininogen as compared to wild-type PLG protein, thereby increasing the
formation of bradykinin (Maas 2019).
Conclusion
The garadacimab development program studied a small number of subjects with FXII-HAE
(N=3) and PLG-HAE (N=3) in a phase 2 trial (2001) at a dosage higher than that included in the
approved labeling. Of the subjects studied, 2/3 subjects with FXII-HAE and 0/3 with PLG-HAE
responded to garadacimab (defined as a ≥50% reduction from baseline in HAE attacks). Subjects
with F12 and PLG mutations had variable responses to garadacimab treatment that could
potentially be explained by emerging mechanistic data from in vivo studies. Given the extremely
small sample size, lack of a control arm, and heterogeneity in disease genotype and phenotype, it
is difficult to draw conclusions regarding the response to garadacimab treatment in the subset of
subjects with Type III HAE. Therefore, no statement regarding patients with Type III HAE will
be included in the PI. A broad HAE indication is reasonable for garadacimab, provided that
language in the labeling describes that approval is supported only by results in subjects with
Type I and Type II HAE. This approach is consistent with prior approvals and labeling for HAE
prophylactic therapies. Further studies of whether subsets of subjects with Type III HAE respond
to FXIIa inhibition with garadacimab, particularly PLG-HAE, are needed. Given that HAE
patients typically receive healthcare from subspecialists with expertise in angioedema, academic
research in the published literature may be the most efficient method for acquiring and
disseminating information about differential treatment effects of garadacimab and other
approved HAE products in patients with Type III HAE with specific mutations.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
Prolongation of aPTT was observed in nonclinical studies conducted with multiple nonclinical
test species (e.g., monkeys, rabbits). There was no evidence of bleeding events in these
nonclinical studies.
46
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Garadacimab is a first-in-class inhibitor of activated FXIIa. FXIIa initiates the contact system
and the intrinsic blood coagulation system. Based on its mechanism of action, potential risks
include bleeding, thromboembolism, and abnormal coagulation laboratory values; however,
patients with genetic FXII deficiency do not typically exhibit a bleeding tendency (see
Section 7.7.1 for a discussion). These risks were monitored during the clinical trial and will be
discussed in this review.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Not applicable. This drug is not yet commercially available in any country.

The clinical data scientists and clinical reviewer performed independent safety analyses using R
Studio 4.1.3, JMP, MAED, and Office of Computational Science Analysis Studio software
programs. The safety assessment is primarily based on data from trial 3001 with support from the
OLE study 3002 and phase 2 dose-ranging trial 2001. Given the differences in design and
duration across trials, safety data were not pooled, but reviewed from each trial independently.
All safety analyses were conducted in the safety population, which consisted of any subject who
received at least one dose of garadacimab. For both trial 3001 and study 3002, the safety
population and the ITT population were the same. The safety results are presented in
Sections 7.6.1 and 7.6.2 for trial 3001 and OLE study 3002, respectively. A summary of safety
data from trial 2001 is provided; however, results are not presented in detail within this review
given the short treatment duration and small number of subjects who received the proposed dose
of garadacimab in the trial.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
No data quality or integrity issues were identified in the submitted datasets or CRFs. Definitions
of adverse events (AEs) and serious adverse events (SAEs) were consistent with Code of Federal
Regulations 312.32. The Applicant coded AEs using the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory
Activities version 25.1 and determined AE severity using the mild, moderate, and severe grading
scale (see Section 15.1.7 for definitions). The Applicant’s translation of verbatim terms to the
Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities preferred terms (PTs) for the events reported in the
trial was reviewed and found to be acceptable. Safety monitoring in the clinical program was
adequate to inform the risks of garadacimab. See Sections 15.1.6, 15.2.2, and 15.3.2 for the
schedules and review of safety assessments.
Overall Exposure
Table 23 represents exposure to the proposed 200 mg dose of garadacimab in the HAE
development program. Across the three trials, 172 subjects with HAE received at least one
200 mg dose of garadacimab, and there was >1 year of exposure to garadacimab in the majority
of subjects. Overall, the safety database is of sufficient size and duration for a rare disease such
as HAE to assess the safety of garadacimab for the proposed indication.

7.6.1.2. Deaths, Trial 3001
There were no deaths in trial 3001.
7.6.1.3. Serious Adverse Events, Trial 3001
In trial 3001, there was one SAE. A 31-year-old female with Type I HAE in the garadacimab
treatment group experienced a severe HAE laryngeal attack on Study Day 99, resulting in
hospitalization. The subject reported sudden onset of globus sensation and difficulty swallowing.
She self-treated with 3,000 IU of C1-INH and received icatibant 30 mg in the emergency
50
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

department. No further on-demand treatment was administered during the hospitalization and her
vitals remained stable. She was discharged from the hospital the following day and continued to
receive study drug without interruption. She completed trial 3001 and continued treatment in
OLE study 3002. As many HAE patients continue to experience breakthrough attacks despite
prophylaxis therapy, this event is likely due to the underlying condition rather than an adverse
reaction related to garadacimab treatment.

There were no TEAEs that led to treatment discontinuation in trial 3001.

Adverse reactions of abdominal pain, nasopharyngitis, and back pain were consistent with the

infections, a total of four garadacimab-treated subjects reported five events, all of which were
nonserious and mild or moderate in severity. None of the infections appear to have been
complicated as all resolved following treatment with oral antibiotics. None of the events led to
garadacimab treatment discontinuation. Although there is an imbalance in the number of
bacterial infections, the lack of consistent pattern to the type/anatomical location of infection or
presumptive causative microorganism makes it difficult to draw conclusions regarding drug
causality. Overall, considering the lack of a consistent microorganism or pattern of infection, that
none of the infections were serious or severe in nature, and the lack of a plausible mechanism of
action, this imbalance does not significantly alter the benefit/risk assessment, and this FMQ term
does not warrant inclusion in labeling.

showed small numerical differences in frequency between treatment groups. All of the events
were mild or moderate in severity. There were no concerning safety signals that significantly
altered the risk of garadacimab in the HAE population.
Due to the small sample size, a cutoff of 7% (or 3) subjects in the garadacimab arm reporting the
same or similar TEAE was used as the threshold for inclusion in the draft labeling.

7.6.1.6. Adverse Events of Special Interest, Trial 3001
Prespecified AEs of special interest (AESIs) included thromboembolic events, bleeding events,
severe hypersensitivity reactions and anaphylaxis. Analyses of AEs by PT, Standard Medical
Dictionary for Regulatory Activities Query (SMQ), and FMQ revealed no severe or lifethreatening/serious AESIs or AESIs that led to treatment discontinuation. The individual AESIs
are discussed in more detail under key safety review issues (Section 7.7).
7.6.1.7. Laboratory Values and Vital Signs, Trial 3001
Routine clinical testing (hematology, serum chemistry with liver function tests, and coagulation)
were collected at baseline, Month 3, and Month 6 during the Treatment Period. The results of the
coagulation studies are discussed in Review Issues in Section 7.7.1. Otherwise, there was no
significant clinical difference between the garadacimab and placebo groups in laboratory
parameters during the Treatment Period. Additionally, no clinically meaningful changes in mean
or median vital signs (systolic blood pressure, diastolic blood pressure, heart rate, respiratory
rate, temperature) were observed with garadacimab treatment compared to placebo across trial
visits.
7.6.1.8. Liver Function Tests, Trial 3001
Liver function tests including alanine aminotransferase (ALT), aspartate aminotransferase
(AST), alkaline phosphatase (ALP), and total bilirubin were collected from subjects at baseline,
Month 3 and Month 6 during the 6-month Treatment Period. Subjects had an additional lab
collection on Day 1 if baseline labs were collected more than 90 days prior to the first dose of
garadacimab administration. There were no potential Hy’s law cases in the program. One subject
in the garadacimab treatment group was noted to have isolated elevations of ALP; the values
were <3× upper limit of normal (ULN). None of the AST/ALT or ALP or total bilirubin values
exceeded 3× ULN. No significant difference in ALT, AST, ALP, and total bilirubin were found
between the garadacimab and placebo groups.
7.6.1.9. Safety Subgroup Analyses Trial 3001
Subgroup analyses by age, sex, race, and geographic region revealed no major differences in
safety; however, the number of subjects within certain subgroups was small. Detailed analyses of
TEAEs by subgroup are located in Section 18.

7.6.2. Safety Results, Study 3002
7.6.2.1. Study Design, Study 3002
Study 3002 is an ongoing phase 3b, single arm, OLE study evaluating the long-term safety of
garadacimab 200 mg SC every month in subjects with Types I, II, and III HAE. The trial
enrolled rollover subjects who completed trial 2001 or 3001 as well as garadacimab-naïve
subjects. For additional details regarding screening and the Run-in Period of study 3002, see
53
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
Key Exclusion Criteria (for Garadacimab-Naïve Subjects)
1. Concomitant diagnosis of another form of angioedema, such as idiopathic or acquired or
recurrent angioedema associated with urticaria.
2. Use of C1-INH products, androgens, antifibrinolytics or other small-molecule medications
for routine prophylaxis against HAE attacks ≥2 weeks before the first day of the
Run-in Period.
3. Use of monoclonal antibodies (including lanadelumab) 3 months before the first day of the
Run-in Period.
4. Female subject’s use of estrogen-containing medications with systemic absorption (e.g., oral
contraceptive or hormonal replacement therapy) within 4 weeks prior to the Run-in Period.
7.6.2.2. Subject Disposition, Study 3002
Subject disposition as of the cutoff date of February 13, 2023, is summarized in Table 27. As the
study is ongoing, the majority of subjects, 146 (90%), are continuing to receive garadacimab
treatment. A total of 79 garadacimab-naïve subjects consented to participate in study 3002, two
subjects discontinued the study prior to the Run-in Period and eight subjects discontinued during
the Run-in Period. The most common reason for discontinuation during the Run-in Period was
an insufficient number of HAE attacks (five subjects).
