7.6.2.8. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 3002
The review analyzed the AE dataset for TEAEs occurring in ≥5% of the total subjects by PT, as
shown in Table 32. Similar to the results from trial 3001, nasopharyngitis/upper respiratory
infection was one of the most common TEAEs. COVID-19 infection, however, was the most
common TEAE. The high frequency of COVID-19 events is not unexpected given the timing of
study initiation on March 29, 2021, during the midst of the COVID-19 pandemic, and these
events are unlikely to be related to garadacimab. The majority of TEAEs were mild or moderate
in severity for both rollover and garadacimab-naïve subjects (Table 29), which is reflective of
trial 3001. The most common mild or moderate TEAEs were nasopharyngitis and COVID-19.
The reported severe TEAEs were diarrhea, tendonitis, nasopharyngitis, toothache, headache,
pulpitis, gastroenteritis, mood swings, and COVID-19. The number of subjects who reported
each TEAE was small, one or two subjects each. All the severe TEAEs resolved without
changing the dose or stopping garadacimab treatment (except for mood swing, as described in
Table 31).

7.6.2.9. Laboratory Findings and Vital Signs, Study 3002
Routine clinical testing, including hematology, serum chemistry with liver function tests, and
coagulation studies, was performed every 3 months. Although study 3002 lacked a comparator
group, lab and vital sign findings while on treatment did not detect any new safety signals. A
discussion of the abnormal coagulation laboratory values can be found under Review Issues in
Section 7.7.1.
Figure 11 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of drug-induced liver injury. There
were no potential Hy’s law cases. Two subjects reported a maximum AST or ALT >5× ULN;
however, both subjects had elevated AST and/or ALT at screening and confounding factors such
as prior danazol use and past medical history of alcohol use and hepatic steatosis. The AST and
ALT levels for these two subjects fluctuated over time with no apparent correlation to
garadacimab administration.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Abnormal Coagulation Labs and Risk of Bleeding
or Thromboembolism
Issue
Because of its mechanism of action, garadacimab has the potential to cause prolongation of
coagulation parameters and result in bleeding or thromboembolic AEs.
Background
Garadacimab is a monoclonal antibody that inhibits the catalytic subunit of FXIIa. FXII is the
first component of the intrinsic coagulation pathway and is typically activated on contact with
substances that reflect tissue injury or damage, such as nucleic acids, heparin, collagen, and
polyphosphates, to promote coagulation. Although inhibition of FXIIa might theoretically cause
abnormal coagulation and a predisposition to bleeding, patients with genetic FXII deficiency
demonstrate prolonged aPTT, but rarely exhibit a bleeding tendency. On the contrary, some
literature suggests that FXII deficiency is associated with an increased risk of thrombosis;
however, the data are inconclusive.
Assessment
Prolongations of aPTT were observed in nonclinical studies conducted in monkeys and rabbits,
as well as in human subjects in the phase 1 and 2 clinical trials. In trial 3001 and OLE
study 3002, the Applicant prospectively monitored coagulation parameters and bleeding or
thromboembolic events as AESIs. Subjects with clinically significant bleeding due to
coagulopathy or thrombotic disorders were excluded from enrollment, but subjects were allowed
to receive anticoagulants (i.e., Factor X inhibitor), antiplatelet medications, or tranexamic acid as
concomitant medications during the trials.
Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time
The aPTT was obtained every 3 months during trial 3001 and study 3002. In trial 3001, 3 of 39
subjects in the garadacimab treatment group had aPTT prolongation (normal range 24.3 to 43.4),
as shown in Table 35. Two of the three subjects had a solitary occurrence of aPTT elevation that
self-resolved without garadacimab dose modification or treatment discontinuation. None of the
subjects experienced clinical manifestations of coagulation pathway abnormalities. Subject
(b) (6) reported taking apixaban (Factor X inhibitor) for a history of bilateral pulmonary
embolism. Otherwise, aPTT prolongations did not correspond to concomitant medication use.

65
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
thromboembolic events occurred. The broad and narrow FMQs for hemorrhage identified two
TEAEs in two subjects in the garadacimab treatment group (Table 37). Both cases self-resolved
and are more accurately characterized as injury-induced bruising rather than representative of an
increased risk for spontaneous hemorrhage or bleeding.
Similarly, in study 3002, there were no thromboembolic events reported. The most commonly
reported bleeding related TEAE in study 3002 was contusion (bruising), reported in four
subjects. The other bleeding related TEAEs, each reported by one subject, consisted of epistaxis,
injection site bruising, injection site hematoma, intermenstrual bleeding, and vaginal bleeding.
The female subjects who reported intermenstrual bleeding and vaginal bleeding were both
<50 years of age. All bleeding related TEAEs were rated as mild in severity, associated with
normal coagulation laboratory values, and did not result in treatment discontinuation.
In trial 2001, one subject who received garadacimab 600 mg Q4 weeks (with a 300 mg IV
loading dose) experienced mild spontaneous epistaxis that self-resolved the same day. Treatment
with garadacimab continued at the same dose with no further bleeding. There was a single TEAE
of vessel puncture site thrombosis in the 200 mg Q4 week group that is unlikely related to study
drug.
Lastly, the review team also examined concomitant use of anticoagulants, antiplatelet or bloodthinning medications and identified four subjects with concomitant medication use. One subject
(b) (6) ), described above, was taking apixaban. Two subjects were on tranexamic
(ID
acid (antiplasmin) and one subject was on ticagrelor (antiplatelet). None of the four subjects
reported a bleeding event, and only the subject taking apixaban had abnormal coagulation
laboratory markers.

Andembry / garadacimab
Conclusion
The prolongations in aPTT and PT coagulation parameters associated with garadacimab
treatment do not exhibit an E-R relationship and appear to be asymptomatic without an increased
risk for bleeding or thromboembolic adverse reactions. No safety signal emerged for bleeding or
thromboemboli irrespective of coagulation markers. Prolongation of coagulation markers without
an increased bleeding risk has also been observed with other approved HAE therapies that target
the intrinsic coagulation pathway. Therefore, this finding might be related to the mechanism of
action of garadacimab and/or interaction between the drug and laboratory test. Information
regarding asymptomatic aPTT/PT prolongation may be useful for healthcare providers and will
be added to the draft PI.

7.7.2. Hypersensitivity Reactions
Issue
(b) (4)

. Does the garadacimab safety data warrant a Warnings and
Precautions statement for hypersensitivity reactions?
Background
Hypersensitivity reactions are frequently associated with injectable products and the therapeutic
class of monoclonal antibodies, regardless of the therapeutic target. As such, hypersensitivity
reactions were prespecified as an AESI in trial 3001 and study 3002.
The Warnings and Precautions section is intended to include serious or clinically significant
adverse reactions in which there is reasonable evidence of causal relationship between the drug
and the AE. Considerations for evidence of causal relationship include frequency of reporting
and whether the rate of the event was greater in drug compared to placebo.
Assessment
The review team analyzed the TEAEs in trial 3001 and study 3002 by PT, narrow and broad
FMQ for hypersensitivity, and SMQ for hypersensitivity. No hypersensitivity TEAEs were
reported with garadacimab treatment in trial 3001. In study 3002, only the FMQ (Broad) and the
SMQ searches yielded TEAEs related to hypersensitivity; however, none of the PTs were
specific or clinically relevant to an immediate hypersensitivity reaction (i.e., allergic rhinitis,
eczema, pruritus).
Conclusion
Immediate hypersensitivity reactions would not be an unexpected safety signal; however, no
events occurred in trial 3001 or study 3002. Per labeling policy, Warnings and Precautions are
reserved for observed, rather than theoretical, risks except in the case of drug class labeling.
(b) (4)
Hypersensitivity is not considered class labeling for monoclonal antibodies.

70
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab

7.7.3. Injection Site Reactions
Issue
ISRs are a common adverse reaction from SC injectable drug products and were observed during
(b) (4)
the garadacimab development program. The Applicant proposes to include ISRs
in Section 6 of the PI.
Background
In the phase 2, dose-ranging trial 2001 (see Section 15.3), there was a dose-dependent increase in
ISRs. ISRs occurred in one (11.1%), one (12.5%), and four (57.1%) subjects in the 75 mg,
200 mg, and 600 mg dose groups, respectively. Because of the relatively high proportion of ISRs
in the 600 mg dose group, the Applicant carried forward the 200 mg dose for evaluation in
phase 3.
Assessment
Analyses of AE datasets for trial 3001 and study 3002 by Local Administration Reaction FMQ
(Narrow and Broad) are shown in Table 38 and Table 39. The narrow and broad search queries
produced the same results. In trial 3001, there was no significant difference in ISRs between the
garadacimab and placebo treatment groups during the placebo-controlled 6-month Treatment
Period.
ISRs were reported by both treatment-naïve and rollover subjects in study 3002, with a higher
frequency in the rollover subjects. Most of the ISRs for the garadacimab-naïve subjects were
reported within the first 6 months of starting garadacimab. One subject in the garadacimab-naïve
treatment group discontinued treatment due to ISR (Section 7.6.2.7).

Conclusion

reactions, information regarding the ISRs observed in study 3002 will be included in the draft PI.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Based on population PK analyses, intrinsic factors including age (12 to 73 years),
, and
race were not found to meaningfully influence the PK of garadacimab after body weight was
introduced as a covariate. No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of
renal impairment or hepatic impairment on the PK of garadacimab. Per the population PK
analysis, renal impairment categories (estimated glomerular filtration rate ≥90 mL/min/1.73 m2
[normal, N=145]; 60 to <90 mL/min/1.73 m2 [mild, N=26]; and 30 to <60 mL/min/1.73 m2
[moderate, N=2]) did not impact the PK of garadacimab. In addition, bilirubin (4 to 13µmol/L;
10th and 90th percentiles, respectively, in the observed dataset) did not have a large impact on
garadacimab clearance.
For additional details on population PK analyses, refer to Section 14.5.

8.1.1. Body Weight
Overall, no dosage adjustments are recommended based on body weight.
In population PK analysis, body weight was identified as a significant covariate impacting the
clearance and volume of distribution of garadacimab. In general, garadacimab exposure is higher
in subjects with lower body weight. The impact of various covariates, including body weight, on
garadacimab AUCtau,ss is shown in Figure 14. The median AUCtau,ss in a subject weighing 105 kg
or weighing 55 kg is predicted to be about 38% lower or 32% higher, respectively, relative to the
reference AUCtau,ss of a typical patient weighing 70 kg. Although differences in exposure are
predicted based on body weight, no clear trend in monthly HAE attack rate by baseline weight
quartile was observed in trial 3001 (Figure 15).
These observations are supported by results from trial 2001, which evaluated various
garadacimab dosing regimens ranging from 75 mg every Q4W to 600 mg SC Q4W over a 13week double-blind Treatment Period. A reduction relative to placebo in the mean timenormalized number of HAE attacks per month was observed for all dosages evaluated with no
apparent dose-response relationship observed from 75 mg to 600 mg. As the observed monthly
HAE attack rate in the 200 mg group was numerically the lowest across all treatment groups, the
200-mg dose was carried over to the phase 3 trial 3001. For additional details, refer to
Section 14.2.4.

Andembry / garadacimab
Population PK simulations following the proposed dosage (i.e., 400 mg SC loading dose, then
200 mg SC Q4W) in adults and adolescents predict 1.33-fold greater Cmax at steady state and
1.36-fold greater AUCtau,ss in adolescents relative to adults (mean [SD] adolescent [N=10,000]
Cmax at steady state and AUCtau,ss=27.3 [14.4] µg/mL and 13,700 [6670] µg·h/mL, respectively;
mean [SD] adult [N=10,000] Cmax at steady state and AUCtau,ss=20.5 [10.0] µg/mL and 10100
[4400] µg·h/mL, respectively) (data derived from Table SYN6, Population
PK/Pharmacodynamic Report).
For additional details on garadacimab PK in adolescents in trial 3001 and study 3002, refer to
Sections 14.2.5 and 14.2.6. For additional details on population PK, refer to Section 14.5.

8.1.2. Type III (FXII/PLG mutation) HAE
Refer to Section 6.3.2.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Dedicated drug-drug interaction studies with garadacimab have not been conducted. As
garadacimab is a monoclonal antibody, direct drug-drug interactions via inhibition or induction
of cytochrome P450 enzymes or transporters are not expected. The Applicant conducted a
population PK analysis to evaluate whether use of concomitant medications had an impact on
garadacimab steady-state exposure. The AUCtau,ss of garadacimab was similar regardless of
whether subjects used analgesics, anti-inflammatory medications, antihistamines, or
antibacterials (Figure 18). Simulated garadacimab exposure was also evaluated depending on
whether subjects used rescue medications for treatment of HAE attacks. Similar garadacimab
AUCtau,ss was observed regardless of whether complement C1-INH or icatibant acetate was used
(Figure 19). Note that Figure 19 also includes box plots for conestat alfa and a category of
“Other Rescue Medicine.” No subjects used conestat alfa while two subjects were categorized as
using other rescue medications. Thus, their impact on steady state garadacimab exposure could
not be evaluated.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

Andembry / garadacimab
fertility parameters, reproductive indices, or sperm assessments. The no observed adverse effect
level (NOAEL) for this study was the HD of 100 mg/kg/dose based on a lack of findings.
In the female fertility and early embryonic development study, female rabbits were treated with
garadacimab (IV doses of 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg q3 days) for 2 weeks prior to mating, during the
mating period, and continuing to Gestation Day 8. Males were not dosed and were assigned to
each group for mating purposes only. No garadacimab-related effects on maternal reproductive
parameters or embryo survival were observed. The NOAEL for female fertility and embryo
survival was the HD, 100 mg/kg/dose.
Embryo-Fetal Development
In the embryo-fetal development study, pregnant rabbits were administered IV doses of 10, 30,
and 100 mg/kg q3 days on Gestation Days 6, 9, 12, 15 and 18. Necropsies were conducted on
Gestation Day 29. There was no effect on treated females observed related to garadacimab
treatment. There were no treatment-related external, visceral, or skeletal major or minor
abnormalities. The NOAEL for maternal and fetal toxicity was concluded to be
100 mg/kg/occasion (HD).
Prenatal and Postnatal Development
In the rabbit pre-and postnatal development study, pregnant rabbits (F0) were treated with
garadacimab from Gestation Day 7 through Lactation Day 38. SC doses of 0, 10 (low dose), 30
(mid dose), and 100 (HD) mg/kg/dose and an IV dose of 100 (HD) mg/kg were administered
once every 5 days on Gestation Day 7, 12, 17, 22, and 27 and Lactation Days 3, 8, 13, 18, 23, 28,
33, and 38. The F1 generation was not directly administered the test article. Prolongation of
aPTT values and toxicokinetic indicated that drug exposure was continuous during the gestation
and lactation periods in F0 maternal animals.
On Gestation Day 29, fetal parturition plasma garadacimab concentrations were 76.6%, 136.6%,
and 40.8% of the maternal concentrations, at SC low dose, SC mid dose, and SC HD,
respectively. At IV HD, maternal and fetal concentrations were approximately equivalent.
Garadacimab was able to cross the placenta.
For F0 dams, no garadacimab-related effects were observed on mortality, clinical signs, body
weight, natural delivery, litter data, or maternal macroscopic observations. The maternal NOAEL
was 100 mg/kg/dose (HD) SC/IV.
For the F1 generation, no garadacimab-related effects were observed on mortality, clinical signs,
body weight, developmental signs, functional observation battery, eye-blink response, presence
of motile sperm, reproductive performance, cesarean section parameters, or preweaning or
postweaning macroscopic observations. In the F1 generation, the NOAEL for fetal development
was 100 mg/kg/dose (HD) SC/IV. The NOAEL for developmental, neurobehavioral, and
reproductive performance in the F1 generation was 100 mg/kg/dose (HD) SC/IV.
Prolongation of aPTT was observed in all four studies with F0 male and female rabbits, but not
associated with any bleeding events. aPTT was not measured in F1 rabbits in the pre-and
postnatal development study.

9. Product Quality
Complete Response With Quality Issues
Although the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed BLA 761367 with
respect to drug substance and immunogenicity assay and has determined that it meets all of those
applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports, the
facility, microbiology and DP disciplines have unresolved quality issues. The DP facility (CSL
Behring GmbH, Marburg, Germany, FEI 3003098680) recommendation is Withhold. The DP
process design is inadequate to minimize potential contamination of the sterile DP, and the DP
process control and process validation data are insufficient to ensure product quality consistency
and process performance consistency. Therefore, from an Office of Pharmaceutical Quality
perspective, this BLA is not deemed ready for approval in its present form until the abovementioned issues are satisfactorily resolved. As such, Office of Pharmaceutical Quality
recommends a complete response action from facility, microbiology, and product quality
perspectives.

9.1. Device or Combination Product
Considerations
The Applicant proposed two to-be-marketed DP presentations for SC administration: 1) a
200 mg/1.2 mL prefilled syringe with needle safety device (PFS-NSD) and a 200 mg/1.2 mL
autoinjector (AI). The Division of Drug Delivery, General Hospital & Human Factors in the
Center for Devices and Radiological Health reviewed the device descriptions, labeling, design
controls, risk analysis, and design verification of the AI and PFS-NSD devices, and determined
that both the PFS-NSD and the AI devices are approvable. The essential performance
requirements including design specifications, verification method, validation, biocompatibility
and stability were found to be acceptable for both the PFS-NSD and the AI (for full details see
the Center for Devices and Radiological Health review submitted on May 28, 2024 and August
21, 2024).
In the pivotal trial 3001, only the PFS-NSD presentation was used; however, the AI presentation
was administered in the OLE study 3002. The Applicant conducted a phase 1, relative
bioavailability bridging study 1004, to compare the PK of garadacimab following a single SC
injection as the PFS-NSD or the AI; the results are presented in Section 14.2.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
Using dextran sulfate activated plasma, garadacimab inhibited FXII activity, resulting in lowered
production of bradykinin.
Garadacimab was shown to only antagonize FXIIa enzymatic activities, displaying 100%
inhibition of FXIIa catalytic activity for both the α and β fragments; this means that zymogen
FXII functions were negligibly impacted. In an in vitro chromogenic assay, garadacimab
inhibited the enzymatic activity of human plasma-derived FXIIa and βFXIIa at equivalent levels
(half-maximal inhibitory concentration ~15nM). Garadacimab produced a prolongation of
activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) clotting times in standard human plasma.
Garadacimab was evaluated for its ability to inhibit FXII activity in blood samples collected
from subjects with hereditary angioedema (HAE). Plasma FXII concentrations and activities
were analyzed in HAE Type I, II, III, and acquired angioedema samples. Elevated FXII activities
were observed in HAE Type I and III samples compared to control samples. No differences in
FXII activities were observed with HAE Type II or acquired angioedema samples. Garadacimab
inhibited FXIIa activity following activation of plasma samples from normal, HAE Types I, II,
III, and C1-esterase inhibitor (C1-INH)-AAE donors with dextran sulfate. Dextran sulfate
activates plasma via initial contact activation of FXII and models initiation of an HAE attack,
leading to bradykinin-mediated swelling. Garadacimab dose-dependently inhibited FXIIa
chromogenic activity, with maximal FXIIa inhibition observed at 10 μg/mL for all normal and
patient plasma samples examined.
In Vivo
In an angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor (captopril)-induced edema murine model
(heterozygous C1-INH-deficient (C1INH+/-) mice treated with 2.5 mg/kg captopril),
garadacimab significantly inhibited the angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor-induced
increase of vascular permeability. Complete ablation of vascular leakage was observed at
garadacimab doses of ≥0.5 mg/kg.
In a murine passive cutaneous anaphylaxis model, FXII-dependent IgE-mediated activation of
mast cells has been shown to increase vascular permeability by heparin-initiated bradykinin
formation. In this model, garadacimab significantly inhibited FXIIa-mediated bradykinin
formation.
Recommendation for Designation of the Established Pharmacological Classification
The recommended established pharmacologic class for garadacimab is “FXIIa inhibitor”
(monoclonal antibody). Garadacimab is a fully humanized, affinity matured monoclonal
antibody that was developed to bind specifically to FXIIa and renders it inactivated, thereby
arresting kallikrein-kinin system activation and contact-mediated thrombosis without affecting
hemostasis. Using an amino acid mutagenesis approach, it was shown that garadacimab binds to
the catalytic site of FXIIa. It acts at a different point in coagulation cascade than the other drugs
approved for the treatment of HAE.
Ecallantide, lanadelumab, and Berotralstat are listed in the FDA established pharmacologic class
Text Phrasing as plasma kallikrein inhibitors. These drugs bind to plasma kallikrein and inhibit
its proteolytic activity. Plasma kallikrein is a protease that cleaves high-molecular-weight
kininogen to generate cleaved high-molecular-weight kininogen and bradykinin.

13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
Safety pharmacology parameters were incorporated into the pivotal repeat-dose 26-week monkey
toxicology study. With intravenous (IV) doses up to 100 mg/kg/week and subcutaneous (SC)
doses up to 200 mg/kg/week, no treatment-related effects on electrocardiogram, blood pressure,
respiration rate, or pulse rate were observed during the 26-week Treatment Period.
In a mouse study evaluating respiratory parameters, garadacimab at IV doses of 3, 10, 30 and
100 mg/kg or SC doses of 60 and 200 mg/kg produced no statistically significant or biologically
relevant effects on respiratory rate, tidal volume, or minute volume at any postdose time point.
The no observed adverse effect levels (NOAELs) were identified as 100 mg/kg (IV) and
200 mg/kg (SC).

13.1.3. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism,
Excretion/Pharmacokinetics
A single-dose pharmacokinetic (PK) study using the IV and SC routes was conducted in
cynomolgus monkeys. The SC bioavailability of garadacimab (6 mg/kg) was ~66% in monkeys.
Mean estimated t1/2 values for garadacimab in the single-dose PK study with monkeys ranged
from 147 to 581 h (IV) and 206 to 424 h (SC).

13.1.4. Toxicology
13.1.4.1. General Toxicology
Repeat-Dose Toxicity
The Applicant characterized the toxicity profile of garadacimab by conducting 4-week mouse
and 26-week monkey toxicology studies with garadacimab using the IV and SC routes.
Immunogenicity in mice precluded conducting longer toxicology studies in this species.
CSL312: Repeat-Dose Toxicity Study by Intravenous and Subcutaneous Administration to
(b) (4) )
CD-1 Mice for 4 Weeks Followed by an 8-Week Recovery Period (Study #
Key Study Findings


In a 4-week toxicity and toxicokinetic (TK) study, CD-1 mice (12/sex/group) received
garadacimab at IV doses of 0, 3, 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg and SC doses of 0, 60, and
200 mg/kg (SC).



There were 23 unscheduled deaths during the study (16 males and 7 females). The deaths of
one HD IV male, one low dose (LD) SC male, and two LD SC females were considered to be
due to the blood sampling procedure. The deaths and/or premature euthanasia of the
remaining animals, primarily in the LD IV group, were attributed to immunogenicity based
upon observed adverse clinical signs, although there were no histopathological findings.

87
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab


Significant prolongations of the aPTT were observed for males (up to 127%) and females (up
to 123%) in all garadacimab treatment groups (IV and SC) relative to control animals at the
end of the 4-week Treatment Period.



The target organs of toxicity were identified as the lymph nodes (axillary and inguinal) and
SC injection sites. An increase in incidence and severity of minimal to slight inflammatory
cell infiltrates at the SC injection sites (dorsal 1 and 2) were observed for females in the 60
and 200 mg/kg SC treatment groups at the end of the Treatment Period.



An increased incidence and/or severity of minimal to slight lymphocyte cellularity in the
axillary and/or inguinal lymph nodes was observed for males and/or females in the 60 and
200 mg/kg SC treatment groups at the end of the 4-week Treatment Period.



Systemic exposure (maximum plasma concentration (Cmax)) and area under the
concentration-time curve (AUC) from 0 to 96 h) to garadacimab increased in a doseproportional manner for males and females from 3 to 100 mg/kg (Day 1) and 10 to
100 mg/kg (Day 29) following IV administration. Moderate accumulation of garadacimab
was observed in the IV dosing groups (1.56 to 3.08) and SC groups (1.38 to 2.33) during the
study period. No clear differences in systemic exposure between males and females were
observed.



The determined NOAEL was the high SC dose of 200 mg/kg.

addition, aPTT was also prolonged in the subcutaneous treatment
groups and peaked at 96 and 48 h postdose in males (up to 2.5-fold
the control) and females (up to 3.1-fold the control), respectively, and
remained extended up to 168 h postdose. The findings were an
expected pharmacological effect of the drug. The findings were not
completely reversible by the end of the 8-week recovery period.
No treatment-related effects on prothrombin times were observed
during the study period.
A slight increase in potassium concentration was observed for
females (up to 27%) in the 60 and 200 mg/kg SC treatment groups
relative to control animals. Clear dose-dependent effects were not
observed. The toxicological significance, if any, of these findings is
unclear. These findings were considered monitorable in a clinical
setting.
A significant decrease in potassium (up to 48%) and chloride (up to
70%) levels were observed for females in the 200 mg/kg/week SC
treatment group relative to control animals at the end of the 26-week
Treatment Period. The toxicological significance, if any, of these
findings is unclear. These findings were considered clinically
monitorable.
Dark areas and thickening observed at the subcutaneous or
intravenous injections sites were observed for several animals at the
end of the Treatment Period. These findings were not considered
toxicologically significant.
No treatment-related effects.

97
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab


The 26-week toxicity study in monkeys did not show any evidence of preneoplastic or
neoplastic findings.



The evidence from the published scientific literature regarding inhibition of activated FXII or
zymogen FXII deficiency does not suggest any carcinogenic risk. Evidence suggests that
inflammatory responses and possible maintenance and progression of cancer could be
reduced by inhibition of FXIIa.



There is general agreement that hereditary FXII deficiency does not usually result in a
hemorrhagic diathesis. FXII knock-out mice are not impacted negatively, although zymogen
FXII has biologic activity in various cells in vivo.



FXII plasma levels are low in patients with lung, gastrointestinal, and colorectal cancer,
suggesting that FXIIa-driven pathways are active in patients with malignancy. Experimental
venous thromboembolism models in mice and studies with cancer patients revealed that
prostate cancer cells and associated exosomes display inorganic polymer polyphosphates on
their plasma membranes. Polyphosphates activate FXII and initiate thrombus formation.
Pharmacologic inhibition of FXIIa protects mice from venous thromboembolism and reduces
thrombin coagulant activity in plasma of patients with prostate cancer.



Garadacimab was shown to only antagonize FXIIa enzymatic activities, displaying 100%
inhibition of FXIIa catalytic activity for both the α and β fragments, meaning that zymogen
FXII functions are negligibly impacted. In contrast, garadacimab did not significantly inhibit
the other serum proteases evaluated. Due to its specific binding to FXIIa without impacting
other coagulation factors or serum proteases, it was considered unlikely that garadacimab
will induce preneoplastic or neoplastic lesions.

Based on the weight of evidence, the Division agreed with the Applicant that a rodent
carcinogenicity study was not technically feasible. Further, there appears to be a low or
negligible carcinogenic potential based on the results of the 26-week monkey study and review
of the published literature regarding chronic inhibition of FXIIa.
Genotoxicity studies with biologics such as garadacimab are not required. As per International
Council for Harmonisation (ICH) guideline, S6(R1) Preclinical Safety Evaluation of
Biotechnology-Derived Pharmaceuticals (June 2011), standard in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity
studies are not applicable to biologics because these molecules are unlikely to interact directly
with DNA or chromosomal material.
13.1.4.3. Reproductive Toxicology
The potential reproductive toxicity of garadacimab was evaluated in fertility and early embryonic
development studies with male and female rabbits, an embryofetal development study with
female rabbits, and a pre- and postnatal development study with female rabbits.
Rabbits were determined to be a pharmacologically relevant species.


Garadacimab binds with high affinity to rabbit FXIIa and βFXIIa as well as FXII zymogen.
Data demonstrated that rabbits are a pharmacologically relevant species for toxicological
investigation (REP-21280).

103
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab


Garadacimab inhibited dextran sulfate-mediated FXIIa activity in diluted plasma from rabbit,
human, and other animal species confirming rabbits as a pharmacologically relevant species
and supporting their use in toxicological investigations (REP-21282).

In correspondence dated March 29, 2019, the Division agreed with the Applicant’s reproductive
toxicity program. Rats were not a pharmacologically relevant species.
Rabbits are not commonly used in male and female fertility studies.


The duration of the spermatogenic cycle is 48 days in the rabbit as compared to 52 days in
rats, which are more commonly used in fertility studies. Treatment of rabbits with
garadacimab once every 3 days for a total of 15 doses should cover the majority of the
duration of the spermatogenic cycle in rabbits. Rabbits were not used in general toxicology
studies with garadacimab, so there were no microscopic evaluations of the testes in longer
studies. The assessment of fertility using male rabbits appears to be generally acceptable as
the fertility study covered the majority of the spermatogenic cycle.



Female rabbits differ from female humans in that they do not have an estrus cycle. Females
ovulate once they are mounted by a male (induced ovulation). The female rabbit can become
impregnated any time, although it is not recommended that females be bred before 4 weeks
of age. The assessment of fertility using female rabbits appears to be generally acceptable,
although potential effects of estrus cycle could not be determined.

Rabbits are commonly used in embryofetal development studies.
Pre-and postnatal development studies are not commonly conducted with female rabbits. The
Applicant appropriately adjusted dosing to cover the duration of the gestation and lactation
periods in rabbits. Monitoring of the growth and development of the F1 generation was also
appropriately adjusted for the use of rabbits.
Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Studies Conducted With Male and Female
Rabbits
The 26-week repeat-dose monkey study was conducted in sexually immature male and female
animals, and estrous cycling was not monitored in females. Therefore, an assessment of the
effects of garadacimab on male and female fertility was not possible from this study. The
Applicant conducted fertility and early embryonic development studies with male and female
rabbits.

Andembry / garadacimab


As expected for the pharmacological mechanism of action of garadacimab, an increase in
aPTT was observed during treatment. The increase persisted until the end of the observation
period (11 days after cessation of treatment). The aPTT prolongation suggested FXIIa
inhibition at all dose levels tested. Prolongation of aPTT was not associated with any
bleeding events.



Systemic drug exposures were continuous during the period of organogenesis from Gestation
Days 6 to 18 based on TK and aPTT data.



There was no evidence of maternal toxicity related to garadacimab treatment. There were no
effects of garadacimab treatment of does on F1 offspring at all dose levels tested (e.g., no
evidence of fetal harm). There were no treatment-related effects on fetal body weights and
placental weights.



There were no treatment-related external, visceral, or skeletal major abnormalities.



Increased numbers of thoracolumbar vertebrae (20), a minor abnormality, were observed for
fetuses in all garadacimab-treated groups. This finding was not reported in the laboratory
historical control data.



The NOAEL for maternal and fetal toxicity was concluded to be 100 mg/kg/occasion (HD).

13.1.4.4. Other Toxicology/Specialized Studies
Cross-Reactivity Study in Human Tissues and Selective Cynomolgus Monkey Tissues
(b) (4) )
(Study #
The aim of this GLP study was to assess the potential cross-reactivity of garadacimab with
specified tissues from human and cynomolgus monkey origin. Garadacimab (5, 2.5, and
1.25 µg/mL) and an isotype control antibody (5 and 2.5 µg/mL) were applied to a full panel of
normal human and cynomolgus monkey tissues. Specific staining was observed with
garadacimab in many human tissues; with strongest staining intensities observed in liver, bone
marrow, lung, spinal cord, cerebral cortex, and eye (optic nerve). No staining was observed with
garadacimab on human ovary, cervix, striated muscle, colon, ileum, thyroid, and endothelium.
Specific staining in cynomolgus monkeys was predictive of staining in human tissues, with the
exception of parathyroid, where only specific staining was observed in human tissues.
Local Tolerance and Bioequivalence Study of Two CSL312 Formulations Following
Subcutaneous Administration in Rabbits (Study # (b) (4) )
Key Study Findings


In the GLP-compliant local tolerance study, two groups (3 sex/group, around 5 weeks old) of
New Zealand white rabbits were treated with two formulations of garadacimab (batch no.
834101076 or batch no. 8120000014) SC on the right side (dorsal skin) of the animals on test
days 1 and 8. All animals were treated with 0.9% NaCl solution as negative control on the
left side. Following an observation period of 11 days after administration, all animals were
sacrificed and the injection sites were examined macro- and microscopically.



There were no signs of toxicity evident from clinical signs, body weights, macroscopic
changes, and microscopic changes.



In group 1, lymphohistiocytic infiltration (3/3 males), neutrophilic granulocytes in the
subcutis (2/3 males), and hemorrhage (3/3 females) were noted on the right side
(garadacimab-treated). In group 2, lymphohistiocytic infiltration (1/3 males, 1/3 females),
neutrophilic granulocytes in the subcutis (1/3 males, 1/3 females), and hemorrhage (1/3
females) were noted on the right side (garadacimab treated).



The two formulations showed similar PK profiles.



Repeated SC administration of two formulations of garadacimab on 2 days was well tolerated
and did not reveal any test item-related changes at the injection sites.

Extractables and Leachables Studies With the Container Closure System
Garadacimab was developed as a combination product incorporating a prefilled syringe which
contains 200 mg of the formulated garadacimab active substance, defined as the garadacimab
semifinished product 200 mg. Extractables and leachables studies were conducted with the
(b) (4)
container closure system which were evaluated (see review dated June 5, 2024).

Andembry / garadacimab
The proposed to-be-marketed (TBM) drug products for SC administration include a
200 mg/1.2 mL prefilled syringe with needle safety device (PFS-NSD) and a 200 mg/1.2 mL
autoinjector (AI). The TBM formulation is a solution with a garadacimab concentration of
170 mg/mL. The TBM formulation in the PFS-NSD presentation was used in studies 1003, 1004,
and 3002 and trial 3001. The AI presentation was used in studies 1004 and 3002.
Across all clinical studies, two drug product formulations have been evaluated, including a
100 mg/mL solution formulation (vialed presentation), and the TBM 170 mg/mL solution
formulation. The two formulations contain the same excipients at the same concentration and
only differ in garadacimab concentration. The 100 mg/mL formulation was used in studies 1001,
and 1003 (IV arms only) and trial 2001. Direct clinical data comparing garadacimab exposure
following administration of the two formulations are not available. The Applicant’s rationale to
support equivalence of the formulations is based on the following:


A single dose nonclinical PK study in rabbits comparing the two formulations at a dose of
20 mg/kg showing a similar PK profile.



A comparison of the steady state trough concentration across trials 2001 (100 mg/mL) and
3001 and study 3002 (170 mg/mL) using population PK indicated that formulation has no
impact on garadacimab PK.

Garadacimab steady state trough concentrations following a 200-mg dose, stratified by
formulation, are shown in Figure 20. The data show that the median steady state trough
concentrations are slightly higher using the 170 mg/mL formulation (visually estimated at about
7500 ng/mL) relative to the 100 mg/mL formulation (visually estimated at about 7000 ng/mL).
However, there is large overlap in the distributions based on the observed data.
The mean (SD) steady-state trough concentrations in trial 2001 among subjects who were
randomized to receive 200 mg SC every 4 weeks (Q4W) ranged from 6414 (2728) to 7001
(1715) ng/mL in Treatment Period 1; and 5718 (2271) to 7656 (2728) ng/mL in
Treatment Period 2 (data derived from Table 14.2.2.6.4, Clinical Study Report [CSR]) for
trial 2001). Mean (SD) steady state trough concentrations in trial 3001 ranged from 8087 (4277)
to 8692 (3934) ng/mL. Mean (SD) steady-state trough concentrations in study 3002 ranged from
7429 (4759) to 7882 (4276) ng/mL. Overall, garadacimab trough concentrations at steady state
overlap between the 100 mg/mL formulation and the 170 mg/mL formulation.

PK Analysis
The 48 subjects in the PK analysis set comprised those who received one dose of garadacimab
and for whom at least one concentration value was reported. PK parameters were estimated using
noncompartmental analyses.
PK Results
Note that the following PK results from study 1001 should be interpreted with caution due to
limitations of in-study analysis. In each of 14 runs (~10% of passing runs), the mean
concentration for one of the quality control (QC) levels fell outside the acceptance criteria.
Replicate QC data were not provided. Therefore, the reviewer is unable to verify whether the
acceptance criteria were met based on at least 50% of QCs per QC level falling within ±20% of
nominal values (Table 91). The impact to the reported PK results is unclear. Therefore, results
should be interpreted with caution.
Mean (SD) garadacimab concentration-time profiles by dose group for the IV and SC cohorts are
shown in Figure 25 and Figure 26, respectively. PK parameters by dose and route of
administration are shown in Table 69. Following single IV infusions of garadacimab, plasma
concentrations generally peaked at the end of infusion, approximately 1 h. However, the median
Tmax in the 0.1 mg/kg IV cohort was approximately 3.5 h. In SC cohorts, the median Tmax ranged
from 5.5 to 7 days. Across all IV and SC cohorts, the mean half-life ranged from 14 to 20 days.

Andembry / garadacimab
Treatment Period 1, which lasted for approximately 13 weeks. The first 32 subjects with C1-INH
HAE (i.e., Type I or II HAE) were randomized 1:1:1:1 to one of the following treatments:


Garadacimab: 40 mg IV loading dose, then 75 mg SC Q4W beginning 1 week later.



Garadacimab: 100 mg IV loading dose, then 200 mg SC Q4W beginning 1 week later.



Garadacimab: 300 mg IV loading dose, then 600 mg SC Q4W beginning 1 week later.



Placebo IV, then placebo SC Q4W beginning 1 week later.

These doses were selected based on a PK/PD model developed to quantify the relationship
between garadacimab concentrations and FXIIa-mediated kallikrein activity from data in healthy
subjects in study 1001. Based on the model, dosages of 75, 200, and 600 mg Q4W were
predicted to result in at least 75% of subjects reaching percentage inhibitions of FXIIa-mediated
kallikrein activity of ≥30, ≥ 50, and ≥90%, respectively. IV loading doses were selected to yield
exposures similar to those after SC doses based on approximate 50% bioavailability, but with
achievement of earlier Tmax. The Q4W dosing interval was based on an observed 19-day half-life
in study 1001.
After the first 32 subjects with C1-INH HAE were randomized, up to an additional 8 subjects
were assigned to an open-label arm in which all subjects received garadacimab at a dosage of
400 mg SC every 2 weeks (Q2W). These subjects did not receive an initial IV loading dose.
Up to 10 eligible subjects with FXII/plasminogen (PLG) HAE (i.e., Type III HAE with normal
C1-esterase inhibitor and genetically confirmed factor XII or PLG gene mutation) were assigned
to a separate open-label arm in which all subjects received garadacimab beginning with a 300 mg
IV loading dose followed by 600 mg SC Q4W beginning 1 week later.
All subjects in Treatment Period 1 were eligible for participation in Treatment Period 2, which
was conducted in an open-label manner and lasted for at least 44 weeks. Subjects were assigned
to treatment as follows:


C1-INH HAE subjects who received 75 mg SC Q4W or received open-label 400 mg SC
Q2W in Treatment Period 1 were randomized 1:1 to 200 or 600 mg SC Q4W.



C1-INH HAE subjects who received 200 mg SC Q4W in Treatment Period 1 continued on
200 mg SC Q4W.



C1-INH HAE subjects who received 600 mg SC Q4W in Treatment Period 1 continued on
600 mg SC Q4W.



FXII/PLG HAE subjects who received 600 mg SC Q4W in Treatment Period 1 continued on
600 mg SC Q4W.

All C1-INH HAE subjects who were receiving the 600-mg dose in Treatment Period 2 had their
dose reduced to 200 mg SC Q4W at the next scheduled study visit, effective with Protocol
Amendment 2.
IV and SC doses were administered using a 100 mg/mL formulation in a vial presentation. In
blinded arms, doses were volume-normalized to maintain the blind. IV doses were given as an
infusion at a volume of 3 mL over 3 min. SC doses were administered at a volume of 6 mL in
Treatment Period 1, or at a volume of 2 mL or 6 mL for doses of 200 or 600 mg, respectively, in
143
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
Treatment Period 2. The preferred site of injection for SC doses was the abdomen, although
another anatomic location could have been used at the discretion of the investigator.
The study enrolled male or female subjects aged 18 to 65 years with a diagnosis of C1-INH HAE
or FXII/PLG HAE.
Routine prophylaxis to prevent HAE attacks with the use of C1-INH products or antifibrinolytics
was prohibited during the Run-in Period and during the Treatment Periods. In addition, the
following were prohibited during the 3 months before Screening and at any time during the
study: angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors, and anticoagulants or antiplatelets.
Permitted therapies included prescribed medications required for management of acute or
chronic medical conditions, and therapies to treat any AEs experienced during the study. Oral
contraceptives or hormonal replacement therapy were permitted if taken at a stable dose in the
3 months prior to Screening. On-demand HAE therapy was permitted at any time during the
study for the treatment of HAE attacks, including plasma-derived or recombinant C1-INH,
icatibant, and ecallantide.
For additional details on the design of Study 2001, refer to Section 15.3.
PK, PD, and Immunogenicity Sampling
Treatment Period 1
C1-INH HAE and FXII/PLG HAE Subjects Receiving Drug Q4W

no sample would be collected at the follow-up visit on Day 180.

Section 16.1 for additional details.
All the 20 subjects assigned to 200 mg SC Q4W completed Treatment Period 2. Two of these
subjects had doses uptitrated to 400 mg Q4W during the study. Note that per protocol, dose
increases were allowed at the discretion of the Investigator for subjects who experienced ≥3
HAE attacks within an 8-week period. A third subject who had originally been assigned to
receive 600 mg SC Q4W was downtitrated to 200 mg SC Q4W and later uptitrated to 400 mg SC
Q4W. The median (range) time to dose uptitration for all subjects was 142 (63, 167) days.
Of 18 subjects assigned to 600 mg SC Q4W, 16 completed Treatment Period 2, with two
discontinuations prior to downtitration. Of a total of 18 subjects originally assigned to 600 mg
SC Q4W, 16 were downtitrated to a dose of 200 mg Q4W, 12 of whom were downtitrated due to
Protocol Amendment 2. Of the four subjects who were downtitrated to 200 mg Q4W prior to
Protocol Amendment 2, one subject had the dose decreased remotely following remote reconsent
before the next scheduled visit. The remaining three subjects had doses reduced as action taken
due to AEs as follows:

Dose Response for Efficacy
The primary endpoint of the study was the time-normalized number of monthly HAE attacks on
treatment with 200 or 600 mg garadacimab compared to placebo in Treatment Period 1. The
time-normalized number of HAE attacks per month in subjects with C1-INH HAE by dose
cohort in Treatment Period 1 is shown in Table 78. A reduction relative to placebo in the mean
time-normalized number of monthly HAE attacks was observed for all dosages evaluated in
Treatment Period 1. No additional numerical reduction in the time-normalized number of
monthly HAE attacks was observed in the 600 mg Q4W and 400 mg Q2W dosing groups
compared to the 200 mg Q4W dosing group. See Section 16.1 for additional details. This trend
continued into Treatment Period 2 in which the mean (SD) time-normalized number of HAE
attacks per month was 0.08 (0.18) and 0.22 (0.35) for subjects receiving 200 mg Q4W and
600 mg Q4W, respectively (data derived from Table 11-6, CSR for Trial 2001). Data from
Treatment Period 2 should be interpreted with caution as it was conducted in an open-label
manner.

For C1-INH HAE subjects who received 400 mg SC Q2W without an IV loading dose, mean
(SD) FXIIa-mediated kallikrein activity decreased from 0.20 (0.05) on Day 1 (predose) to 0.09
(0.07) at Week 3, 0.07 (0.03) at Week 9, 0.04 (0.05) at Week 10, 0.08 (0.03) at Week 11, 0.04
(0.04) at Week 12, and 0.06 (0.04) at Week 13 (data derived from Table 14.2.3.3.2.2, CSR for
Trial 2001). Decreases in activity were observed after the first dose and maintained throughout
the Treatment Period. Note that doses were dispensed on Day 1 and Weeks 3, 5, 7, 9, and 11.
Thus, measurements at Weeks 10 and 12 occurred postdose. The lower mean activity at those
time points may thus be attributed to higher plasma garadacimab concentrations.
Dose-dependent inhibition in FXIIa-mediated kallikrein activity was maintained in
Treatment Period 2. From Week 17 onwards, mean (SD) FXIIa-mediated kallikrein activity
ranged from 0.14 (0.04) to 0.17 (0.06) for the 200 mg SC Q4W arm, and from 0.8 (0.05) to 0.10
(0.05) for the 600 mg SC Q4W arm (data derived from Table 14.2.3.3.2.3, CSR for Trial 2001).
Values observed for the 200 mg SC Q4W arm were similar to baseline levels for all treatment
arms.
Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time
Per the Applicant, the normal range for aPTT values is between 24.3 and 43.4 seconds. During
Treatment Period 1, in the 75 and 200 mg Q4W treatment arms, mean aPTT values post-first
dose fell within this range at all time points (mean [SD] values range from 24.88 [3.23] to 28.26
[3.01] seconds). In these cohorts, maximum values did not exceed the upper limit of normal. No
difference from placebo was apparent.
In the 600 mg Q4W cohort, mean post-first dose values were in general higher than those in the
75 and 200 mg Q4W cohorts (mean [SD] values range from 42.02 [17.8] to 47.95 [29.1]
seconds). In the 600 mg Q4W treatment arms, the mean aPTT was above normal range on
Days 35 and 63 with a mean [SD] value of 47.50 [31.69] seconds. Although mean values did not
exceed the upper limit of normal on Day 91, maximum values indicate that at least some subjects
exceeded the normal range. Per the Applicant, this prolongation in Treatment Period 1 was
observed in three subjects (data derived from Table 14.2.3.3.7.1, CSR for Trial 2001).
In the 400 mg Q2W treatment arm, the mean aPTT was within normal range at all time points.
Maximum values fell within the normal range at all time points except for the Day 85 time point

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

Andembry / garadacimab
(mean [SD] aPTT at this time point was 36.38 [11.65] seconds) (data derived from Table
14.2.3.3.7.2, CSR for Trial 2001).
In Treatment Period 2, the mean aPTT was within the normal range at all time points for the
200 mg Q4W cohort (mean [SD] values range from 26.85 [4.22] to 28.68 [6.62] seconds).
Maximum values exceeded normal range at Week 57. In the 600 mg Q4W cohort, mean values
were in general higher than those in the 200 mg Q4W cohort (mean [SD] values range from
31.33 [10.7] to 44.3 [34.6] seconds). Maximum values exceeded normal range at all time points
(data derived from Table 14.2.3.3.7.3, CSR for Trial 2001).
Immunogenicity Results
No subject treated with garadacimab tested positive for ADAs during Treatment Period 1. One
subject randomized to placebo tested positive for ADAs at week 13 (i.e., the end of
Treatment Period 1). This subject received 200 mg Q4W garadacimab in Treatment Period 2. No
ADAs were detected in subsequent visits.
Note that immunogenicity samples were collected on Day 1, Week 13, and Week 57. Due to the
sampling scheme, subjects with transient ADAs may not have been captured. Regardless, the
data suggest that subjects likely did not have persistent ADAs.
Also note that the drug tolerance of the ADA assay is adequate up to 30 µg/mL garadacimab
(refer to Office of Pharmaceutical Quality executive summary review dated August 26, 2024). At
ADA sample collection time points, there were six subjects with seven samples with
garadacimab concentrations exceeding 30 µg/mL, including three subjects who received 400 mg
Q2W (concentrations of 46.0 to 54.1 µg/mL at Day 85) and three subjects who received 600 mg
Q4W (one with a concentration of 31.4 µg/mL at Day 91, one with a concentration of
43.7 µg/mL at Day 91 and 36.5 µg/mL at Week 57, and one with a concentration of 32.0 µg/mL
at Week 57).

14.2.5. Trial 3001 (Phase 3 Efficacy Trial in Subjects
With Type I and II HAE)
Title
A multicenter, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel-arm trial to investigate the
efficacy and safety of SC administration of garadacimab in the prophylactic treatment of HAE.
Study Design
For a detailed description of the design of trial 3001, refer to Section 6.2.1.
Trial 3001 was designed as a multicenter, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled,
parallel-arm trial in adolescent and adult subjects with HAE. Following a Run-in Period of up to
2 months, subjects were randomized 3:2 to receive either garadacimab or placebo during the
6-month Treatment Period. Subjects on garadacimab received a 400 mg SC loading dose
(administered as 2×200 mg) followed by 200 mg SC once monthly beginning on Day 31.
Randomization was stratified by age (≤17 years or >17 years).
SC doses were administered using a 170 mg/mL solution in 1.2 mL PFS-NSD devices. SC
injections were administered as 1×1.2 mL (200 mg) or 2×1.2 mL (400 mg) in the abdomen.
154
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
The 200-mg dose was selected based on efficacy, safety, PK, and PD observed in
Treatment Period 1 of trial 2001, with support from exposure-response (E-R) modeling. An E-R
model was constructed to simulate HAE attack rates over various garadacimab concentrations.
The median predicted minimum daily average garadacimab concentrations at steady state
following a 200 mg SC once monthly regimen corresponded to 90% relative risk reduction in
HAE attack rate. Increasing the dose beyond 200 mg was not predicted to result in further
reduction in HAE attack rate.
In end-of-Phase Two Meeting Minutes dated October 17, 2020 in DARRTS under IND 139936
(reference ID: 4687429), the Agency noted that the proposed dose and dose interval are
supported by the phase 2 dose-ranging trial (2001), but noted that a different formulation from
that used in 2001 will be used. As a result, the choice of the proposed dose and dosing interval
was left at the risk and discretion of the Applicant. In addition, the Agency also requested that
the Applicant provide justification for use of a loading dose.
The trial enrolled male or female subjects aged ≥12 years with a diagnosis of clinically
confirmed C1-INH HAE (i.e., Type I or II HAE). No subject with FXII/PLG HAE diagnosis
(i.e., HAE with normal C1-esterase inhibitor and genetically confirmed factor XII or PLG gene
mutation) was enrolled in this trial.
For adults, use of C1-INH products, androgens, antifibrinolytics, or other small-molecule
medications for routine prophylaxis against HAE within 2 weeks prior to the Run-in Period was
exclusionary. For adolescents aged 12 to 17 years, use of long-term prophylactic therapy for
HAE before screening was exclusion. Subjects were also excluded if they used lanadelumab
within 3 months prior to the Run-in Period, or use of systemically absorbed estrogen-containing
medications (e.g., oral contraceptives or hormone replacement therapy) or angiotensinconverting enzyme inhibitors within 4 weeks prior to the Run-in Period.
Permitted therapies included prescribed medications required for management of acute or
chronic medical conditions, and therapies to treat any AEs experienced during the trial. Ondemand HAE therapy was permitted at any time during the trial for the treatment of HAE
attacks, including plasma-derived or recombinant C1-INH, icatibant, and ecallantide.
PK, PD, and Immunogenicity Sampling
Blood samples to assess PK and PD (i.e., FXIIa-mediated kallikrein activity) were collected on
Days 1, 31, 62, 91, 121, 151, 182 (Month 7, end of the Treatment Period), and 242 (final
follow-up visit).
Samples for coagulation labs were collected at Screening, predose on Day 1, and on Days 91 and
182 (Month 7, end of the Treatment Period).
Immunogenicity samples were collected on Day 1, and on Days 91 and 182.
Subject Disposition and Demographics
For details, see Section 6.2.4. A total of 76 subjects entered the Run-in Period and 65 subjects
completed the Run-in Period and were randomized to treatment, including 39 who received
garadacimab and 26 subjects who received placebo. One subject in the placebo arm was
randomized to blinded treatment in error and never received study treatment. Of the remaining
64 subjects, 61 completed treatment and 60 completed the study. Three subjects, all in the
155

Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab

14.2.6. Study 3002 (Long-Term Open-Label Extension
Study in Subjects With HAE)
Title
An open-label study to evaluate the long-term safety and efficacy of garadacimab in the
prophylactic treatment of hereditary angioedema.
Study Design
For a detailed description of the study design of 3002, refer to Sections 7.6.2 and 15.2.
Study 3002 is ongoing. An interim CSR was provided with a data cutoff of February 13, 2023
for all subjects, and May 22, 2023 for adolescent subjects (i.e., when 8/10 adolescents had
≥12 months of exposure).
Study 3002 was designed as a multicenter, open-label, phase 3b study in adolescent and adult
subjects with HAE. This study enrolled rollover subjects with HAE from studies 2001 and 3001,
as well as newly enrolled subjects with Type I or Type II HAE. All subjects received
garadacimab. Rollover subjects continued to receive garadacimab at a dosage of 200 mg SC once
monthly. Following a Run-in Period, garadacimab-naïve subjects received a 400 mg SC loading
dose (2×200 mg) followed by 200 mg SC once monthly beginning one month later. Each subject
was to receive garadacimab for at least 12 months.
SC doses were administered using a 170 mg/mL solution in 1.2 mL prefilled syringe or AI
devices. SC injections were administered as 1×1.2 mL (200 mg) or 2×1.2 mL (400 mg) in the
abdomen.
The study enrolled male or female subjects aged ≥12 years with a diagnosis of clinically
confirmed C1-INH HAE (i.e., Type I or II HAE). Criteria regarding washout of prior
medications and use of concomitant medications is similar to that from trial 3001.
PK, PD, and Immunogenicity Sampling
For subjects from 3001 and all naïve subjects, blood samples to assess PK and PD (i.e., FXIIamediated kallikrein activity) were collected on Days 1, 31, 61, and 91; Months 6, 9, 12, 18, 24,
and 30; and at the end-of-treatment visit.
For subjects from 2001, blood samples to assess PK and PD were collected on Days 1 and 91;
Months 6, 12, 18, 24, and 30; and at the end-of-treatment visit.
Additional PK/PD samples were collected in a subset of garadacimab-naïve subjects to
characterize the PK of the loading dose. Following administration of the loading dose on Day 1,
samples were collected on Days 7, 14, 21, and 30.
For adolescent subjects, additional PK/PD samples were collected following the Month 3
(Day 91) dose on Days 3, 7, 14, 21, and 30 (predose prior to the next dose).
Samples for coagulation labs were collected at Screening, predose on Day 1, and on Days 91,
181, 361 (Month 12), 541 (Month 18), 721 (Month 24), 901 (Month 30), and at the end-oftreatment visit. Rollover subjects from 2001 did not have a sample collected on Day 91.

160
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
For all subjects, immunogenicity samples were collected on Day 1, and at Months 6, 12, and 24
and at the end-of-treatment visit.
Subject Disposition and Demographics
For details, see Section 7.6.2. A total of 92 subjects rolled over from trials 2001 and 3001. From
2001, 35 subjects were enrolled, including 2 subjects with FXII HAE diagnosis (normal C1-INH
with FXII gene mutation). From 3001, 57 subjects were enrolled, including 21 subjects who had
received placebo and were therefore considered garadacimab-naïve. A total of 77 subjects with
Type I or II HAE who were not previously enrolled in 2001 or 3001 entered the Run-in Period,
and 69 subjects entered the Treatment Period.
One hundred sixty-one subjects entered the Treatment Period, including 90 who were
garadacimab-naïve and 71 who were previously treated. Of these, 147 subjects are ongoing.
Three subjects completed the study, and 11 prematurely discontinued. One hundred nineteen of
the one hundred sixty-one subjects had at least 12 months of exposure to garadacimab.
Among all subjects, the median (range) body weight at baseline was 78.3 (45.0, 131) kg. The
study enrolled 10 adolescents ranging in age from 13 to 17 years. Five adolescents rolled over
from trial 3001 (n=3 received garadacimab and n=2 received placebo), and the remaining 5 were
newly enrolled garadacimab-naïve subjects. Among adolescents, the median (range) age was
14.5 (13, 17) years, and the median (range) body weight at baseline was 55.7 (45.2, 88) kg.
PK Analysis
All 161 subjects who received garadacimab were included in the PK population, which was
comprised of all subjects with at least one measurable concentration of garadacimab.
Plasma concentrations were summarized by nominal time points and plotted. PK parameters
were derived for the PK subgroup of garadacimab-naïve subjects who had additional samples
taken following the loading dose (n=15). These included Tmax, Cmax, and AUC0-30.
Noncompartmental analysis was not conducted. PK data were included in analyses of population
PK, PK/PD, and E-R.
PK Results
The mean (SD) garadacimab concentration-time profile for all subjects up to the time of data
cutoff is shown in Figure 42. At the time of data cutoff, PK data up to 12 months were available
for 101 subjects. Most subjects (n=140) had data up to 6 months. From Month 3 through
Month 12, mean (SD) steady-state trough concentrations ranged from 7429 (4759) ng/mL to
7882 (4276) ng/mL. Note that data shown include trough concentration data for all subjects (i.e.,
rollover and garadacimab-naïve subjects). Higher plasma concentrations with greater variability
are observed at Month 1 and Month 2 due to some subjects receiving the loading dose.

14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
14.3.1. PK Bioanalytical Methods
Overall, bioanalytical methods to quantitate garadacimab concentrations in clinical study
samples were determined to be acceptable.
Garadacimab concentrations were determined using an enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay.
Briefly, an anti-idiotype antibody specific to garadacimab was coated on a microtiter plate.
Following addition of plasma samples, garadacimab is bound to the anti-idiotype antibody.
Bound garadacimab is detected using a commercial antihuman lambda chain specific IgG
antibody conjugated with horseradish peroxidase. Application of an appropriate substrate
produces a blue color in proportion to the number of complexes formed. The bioanalytical
method was performed in two laboratories:


CSL Research and Clinical Bioanalytics
– Studies 1001 and 1003, and trial 2001
– Procedure RnD-SOP-000053053 (manual method) was used in 1001, 1003, and 2001
– Procedure RnD-SOP-000053165 (automated method) was used in 1001. Partial
validation was conducted to support use of an automated Tecan EVO200 platform. Assay
principle and format is unchanged from the manual method
– The method was revalidated for use in 1003 and 2001 due to exhaustion of stocks and
standards (reported in RnD-VAL-000141302)



(b) (4)

– Study 1004, trial 3001, and study 3002
– Procedure TP80240 was used in 1004 and 3002
– Procedure TP80003 was used in 3001
Because subjects from trial 2001 rolled into study 3002, concordance testing was performed to
compare bioanalytical method performance across the two laboratories.
Note that for the assay used for 1001 (CSL Research), the reported assay range is 1.25 to
100 ng/mL in 1% human plasma based on 100-fold dilution (i.e., 125 to 10,000 ng/mL in PK
samples prior to dilution). For the assay used for 1003 and 2001 (CSL Research) following
167
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5458438

BLA 761367
Andembry / garadacimab
revalidation, the assay range is 1 to 100 ng/mL in 1% human plasma (100 to 10,000 ng/mL in PK
samples prior to dilution).
The assay range for the method used in 1004 and 3002 (TP80240) ranged from 1.01 to
111 ng/mL in 1% human plasma (i.e., 101 to 11100 ng/mL in PK samples prior to dilution). The
assay range for the method used in 3001 (TP80003) ranged from 10.6 to 111 ng/mL in 1%
human plasma (1060 to 11100 ng/mL in PK samples prior to dilution). The validation report for
TP80003 notes that initial evaluations of selectivity at a concentration of 1.01 ng/mL did not
meet acceptance criteria. Thus, the lower limit of quantification for TP80003 was increased to
10.6 ng/mL, a concentration at which selectivity evaluations were acceptable.
Assay procedures TP80240 and TP80003 are very similar, and the key differences include
extension of the standard curve in TP80240 to accommodate a wider analytical range, and
assessment of the lower limit of quantification. The Applicant conducted a full revalidation to
support the wider analytical range. Refer to the Applicant’s Information Request Response dated
February 20, 2024.

In-Study Bioanalysis Reports
Bioanalytical method performance in study 1004 is described in report RnD-SUP-000564586.
The Division submitted a request to the Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance to conduct an
inspection of the bioanalytical site for study 1004. Refer to the Office of Study Integrity and
Surveillance review submitted in DARRTS dated December 5, 2023. Per the Office of Study
Integrity and Surveillance inspection summary, a two-item Form FDA 483 was issued for underreporting of sample reanalysis and inaccurate reporting of study activities for study 1004.
Nevertheless, based on the inspectional findings and the firm’s responses, it was concluded that
the observations were unlikely to have an impact on study data reliability. The following
recommendations were made:


The reported value of one subject sample should be removed due to unacceptable variability



14.3.2. PD Bioanalytical Methods
14.3.2.1. Activated Factor XII-Mediated Kallikrein Activity
To determine activated FXII-mediated kallikrein activity in clinical study samples, a
chromogenic enzymatic assay was used to track inhibitory effects of garadacimab on the
kallikrein-kinin system. Plasma samples are diluted in a reaction buffer containing dextran
sulfate, an activator of the contact system. The chromogenic substrate (S2302), which is
enzymatically cleaved by kallikrein and FXIIa, was used to monitor the amidolytic activity
generated in plasma samples upon activation of the contact system. Substrate cleavage is
monitored at an absorbance of 405 nm (A405) with the change of absorbance per minute as the
assay readout. Control plasma samples in the presence and absence of garadacimab or kallikrein
were included to approximate clinical samples possessing high, medium, and low levels of
amidolytic activity. Note that this assay did not use a calibration standard and thus the accuracy
of the method was not determined. Amidolytic activity generated after ex vivo contact activation
was reported as a continuous variable to track the level of inhibition by garadacimab. This means
that the individual values themselves are not likely to be meaningful.
Note that there are limitations to the PD assay used to measure FXIIa-mediated kallikrein
activity. First, the chromogenic substrate S2302 is not specifically cleaved by FXIIa, but rather is
also cleaved by kallikrein. Although, presumably, inhibition of FXIIa should also inhibit
downstream production of kallikrein. The second is that the method is semiquantitative, as noted
above. Thus, individual values determined from the assay are not likely to be meaningful.
For phase 3 studies, validation was updated for phase 3 drug product and to incorporate frozen
controls rather than controls prepared “in-assay.” Robustness was also assessed for use of
standard cryovials compared to FluidX tubes.


The same assay procedure RnD-SOP-000053087 was used for trials 2001 and 3001, and
study 3002. All assays were performed by CSL Research and Clinical Bioanalytics
– Validation report RnD-VAL-000148888 describes validation of the method in 2001
– Validation report REP-019128 describes validation of the method in 3001 and 3002.

The range of the assay was determined by spiking kallikrein into prekallikrein-deficient plasma
(Table 94 and Table 95). For both validation reports, activity was found to increase at kallikrein
concentrations up to 10 U/mL (RnD-VAL-000148888) or 5 U/mL (REP-019128). The Applicant
hypothesizes that at higher kallikrein concentrations, amidolytic activity is so rapid that the
majority of substrate cleavage occurs prior to initiation of the first absorbance time point of the
kinetic read. Thus, the upper limits of the assay are defined at the maximum activity observed for
each validation. The assay range is 0.003 to 0.371 ΔA405nm/min for RnD-VAL-000148888 and
0.005 to 0.298 ΔA405nm/min for REP-019128. In both reports, FXII-mediated kallikrein activity
was measured in the plasma of subjects with HAE (n=6). In all cases, the mean activity fell
within the assay range.



Study 3002 – the %CV for QCs ranged from 21.3 to 48.0%
– %CV for low controls was 39.6%. Across runs, assay values ranged from 0.004 to 0.015,
a difference of 0.011. The high %CV observed is likely due to the low values which have

In report REP-020155 (in-study report for 3002), HAE sample stability was assessed with
incurred sample analysis using stored samples from trials 2001 (n=31) and 3001 (n=57). Samples
for 2001 were stored for 13 to 18 months, while samples for 3002 were stored for 6 to
13 months. A total of 62.5% (55/88) of retested samples were within 30% difference of the
original value, including 21/31 samples (67.7%) from 2001 and 34/57 samples (59.6%) from
3001. The samples from 2001 were stored for a longer period of time than those from 3001 and
the number of samples with a percent difference <30% was higher in 2001. This suggests that the
method is prone to variability in measurements and the differences observed may not be due to
stability issues.

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
Across clinical studies conducted in subjects with HAE who received garadacimab (n=173), a
total of 5 subjects (2.9%) tested positive for treatment-emergent antidrug antibodies (TE-ADAs).
Two subjects tested positive prior to receiving garadacimab treatment. These subjects exhibited
low ADA titers (i.e., 10). No adolescent subject tested positive for TE-ADAs. Among subjects
who received the proposed dosing regimen of garadacimab (400 mg SC loading dose on Day 1,
then 200 mg SC once monthly) in trial 3001 and study 3002 (n=129; includes 39 garadacimabtreated subjects in 3001, 21 placebo-treated subjects in 3001 who rolled over to 3002, and 69
garadacimab-naïve subjects from 3002), the incidence of TE-ADAs was 3.9 % (5/129). Of six
post-treatment ADA-positive samples, only two samples showed higher titers (160 and 320),
whereas four samples showed lower titers (10). In addition, only one out of five TE-ADA
positive subjects tested positive at more than one time point, indicating that most positive
immunogenicity results were transient. In phase 1 single-dose studies conducted in healthy
subjects (i.e., 1001, 1003, and 1004), no subjects tested positive for ADAs.
Note that the drug tolerance of the ADA assay is adequate up to 30 µg/mL garadacimab (refer to
Office of Pharmaceutical Quality executive summary review dated August 26, 2024). In
trial 3001, no subjects had garadacimab trough concentrations exceeding 30 µg/mL with median
(range) trough concentrations from Day 60 through Day 180 ranging from 7.21 (1.26,
21.3) µg/mL to 8.27 (1.75, 17.9) µg/mL. In study 3002 at ADA sample collection time points,
five subjects had garadacimab concentrations exceeding 30 µg/mL, including two subjects at
Month 6 (concentrations of 34.1 and 37.7 µg/mL), and three subjects at Month 12
(concentrations of 31.9 to 33.4 µg/mL). There were few samples with garadacimab
concentrations exceeding 30 µg/mL. As the ADA incidence was generally low, these
observations are not expected to impact the overall assessment.
A summary of immunogenicity data are provided in Table 97. TE-ADAs were observed in
trial 3001 and study 3002 among subjects receiving the proposed TBM dosage. In trial 3001, one
183

tested positive for ADAs across the garadacimab development program, precluding a definitive
assessment of the impact of ADAs on safety.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Population PK Analysis
14.5.1.1. Executive Summary
The Applicant submitted a population PK report entitled “Population PK-PD Modeling and
Simulation of Garadacimab in Subjects with Hereditary Angioedema” to support the application
of garadacimab for the treatment of adolescents and adults with HAE. The population PK and
PK-PD models in this report were developed using data from two phase 1 studies, one phase 2
study, and two phase 3 studies.
In general, the Applicant’s population PK and PK-PD models adequately characterize the PK/PD
(FXIIa-mediated kallikrein activity) of garadacimab, and the model-predicted real time
concentrations of garadacimab are adequate for use in E-R analysis. In the final model, PK was
described by a two-compartment model with first-order absorption and elimination, and PD was
described by a direct effect sigmoidal maximal-efficacy model. None of the covariates (e.g., age,
sex, race, body weight, renal impairment, Japanese ethnicity, Chinese ethnicity, baseline serum
creatinine, baseline alanine aminotransferase, baseline bilirubin, baseline FXIIa-mediated
kallikrein activity) were likely to have clinically meaningful impact on garadacimab exposure
and PD response. The recommended dosage for garadacimab in patients with HAE is supported
by the population PK/PK-PD analyses results as well as those of the other analyses (i.e., clinical
efficacy, safety, and E-R analyses).

14.5.2. Exposure-Response Analysis
14.5.2.1. ER (Efficacy) Executive Summary
A time-varying Poisson process model with a maximum inhibition (E-R) relationship generally
described the efficacy endpoint (HAE attack rate) in subjects with HAE who received either
garadacimab or placebo in the phase 2 trial (2001) and phase 3 studies (3001 and 3002). None of the
covariates (e.g., age, Japanese, Chinese, male, prior treatment, the use of concomitant medications
[analgesic, anti-inflammatory and antirheumatic, antihistamine, antibacterials] and rescue
medications [plasma-derived and recombinant C1-INH or icatibant], body weight, obesity) were
likely to have clinically meaningful impact on the effectiveness of garadacimab in reducing HAE
attack. Dose adjustments due to the forementioned covariates are not needed.
